Construction of logarithmic cohomology theories I
Doosung Park

Abstract
. We propose a method for constructing cohomology theories of logarithmic schemes with strict normal crossing boundaries by employing techniques from logarithmic motivic homotopy theory over F 1 \mathbb{F}_{1} . This method recovers the K-theory of the open complement of a strict normal crossing divisor from the K-theory of schemes as well as logarithmic topological Hochschild homology from the topological Hochschild homology of schemes. In our applications, we establish that the K-theory of non-regular schemes is representable in the logarithmic motivic homotopy category, and we introduce the logarithmic cyclotomic trace for the regular log regular case.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14F42; Secondary 14A21, 14M25, 19D55

1.
Introduction

A log scheme may be conceptualized as a “scheme with boundary.” Extending a cohomology theory from schemes to log
schemes presents a natural challenge. A cohomology theory formulated
for log schemes can provide valuable insights into both the cohomology
theories of schemes and their extensions.

1.1.
Logarithmic extensions of non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theories

An early example includes the extension of Hodge cohomology
to smooth varieties
X
over a field
k
with a normal crossing
boundary
D
, utilizing the log differential forms

\Omega_{X/k}^{q}(\log D)
. For a smooth variety
U
over
\mathbb{C}
,
one can find a proper smooth variety
X
over
\mathbb{C}
containing

U
as an open subscheme with a normal crossing divisor
D
as its
complement by Hironaka’s resolution of singularities

[
37
]
. Grothendieck
[
34
]
proved that there is a natural
quasi-isomorphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(U)\simeq R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(U^{\mathrm{an}}),

which connects algebraic and
analytic de Rham complexes, and the log differential forms
\Omega_{X/k}^{q}(\log D)

plays an important intermediate role. Subsequently, Deligne, Faltings, Fontaine,
Illusie, and Kato developed the theory of logarithmic geometry,
where
the logarithmic forms
\Omega_{X/\mathbb{C}}^{q}(\log D)
are realized
as differential forms of the associated log scheme.

Another notable example is the extension of topological Hochschild
homology and its related theories to fs log schemes. For a log ring

(A,M)
, which consists of a ring
A
and a monoid
M
equipped with a
map of monoids
M\to A
,
Rognes
[
59
, Definition
8.11]
defined topological Hochschild homology

\mathrm{logTHH}(A,M)
. This admits a cyclotomic structure in the sense of
[
50
, Definition II.1.1]
by
[
42
, Definition 3]
,
which leads to the definitions
of negative topological cyclic homology
\mathrm{logTC}^{-}(A,M)
and
topological cyclic homology
\mathrm{logTC}(A,M)
.

Bhatt, Morrow,
and Scholze
[
7
, Theorem 1.12]
established motivic filtrations
on the
p
-completed ones

\mathrm{THH}(A;\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\text{ }\mathrm{TC}^{-}(A;\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\text{ }\mathrm{TC}(A;\mathbb{Z}_{p})

for quasi-syntomic
rings
A
.
The
0
th graded pieces of
\mathrm{TC}^{-}(A;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and

\mathrm{TC}(A;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
can be identified with Nygaard-completed
prismatic cohomology and syntomic cohomology respectively. Binda,
Lundemo, Park, and Østvær
[
10
, Theorem 1.3]
extended
these filtrations to

\mathrm{logTHH}((A,M);\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\text{ }\mathrm{logTC}^{-}((A,M);\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\text{ }\mathrm{logTC}((A,M);\mathbb{Z}_{p})

for log quasi-syntomic rings
(A,M)
.
Ultimately, many cohomology theories
of schemes admit logarithmic extensions.

One application of these
logarithmic extensions is the construction of Gysin sequences.
For a separated finite dimensional noetherian scheme
S

(which we denote as
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
),
let
\mathrm{Sm}/S
denote the category of smooth
S
-schemes in
\mathrm{Sch}
.
Define
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
as the category of “smooth log smooth fine saturated (fs for short) log schemes,” that is, log smooth fs log schemes over
S
whose underlying schemes are also smooth over
S
.
For
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
, let

\partial X
denote the boundary of
X
, which corresponds to the
strict normal crossing divisor on the underlying scheme
\underline{X}
.
The complement
X-\partial X
is the largest open subscheme of
X
with the trivial log structure.
If
Y\in\mathrm{Sm}/S
and
D
is its strict normal crossing divisor, we can associate
(Y,D)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
whose underlying scheme is
Y
and boundary is
D
using the
Deligne-Faltings log structure
[
52
, Definition III.1.7.1]
.
There exists a strict Nisnevich topology on
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S

[
11
, Definition
3.1.4]
, which is abbreviated as
\mathrm{sNis}
.
If a strict Nisnevich sheaf of spectra
\mathbb{E}
on
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
satisfies

(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance for all integers
n
,
then
\mathbb{E}
is an object of the logarithmic motivic homotopy category
\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
as noted in
[
12
, Remark 2.4.14]
.
According to
[
11
, Theorem 7.5.4]
,
we have the Gysin fiber sequence

\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Th}(\mathrm{N}_{Z}X))\to\mathbb{E}(X)\to\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Bl}_{Z}X)

for every closed immersion

Z\to X
in
\mathrm{Sm}/S
, where
E
is the exceptional divisor on the blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{Z}X
, and
\mathrm{Th}(\mathrm{N}_{Z}X)
is the logarithmic Thom space of the normal bundle
\mathrm{N}_{Z}X
in the sense of
[
11
, Definition 7.4.3]
.
Binda, Lundemo, Merici, and Park
[
8
]

utilized this method to construct the Gysin sequences for Nygaard-completed prismatic cohomology and syntomic cohomology.

So far, we have discussed cohomology theories of fs log schemes that require additional data for their definitions.
All of
these examples are also non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant.

1.2.
Logarithmic extensions of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theories

On the other hand,
for an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant Nisnevich sheaf of spectra
\mathbb{E}
such as K-theory on
\mathrm{Sm}/S
with regular noetherian
S
,
we can simply define

\mathbb{E}(X):=\mathbb{E}(X-\partial X)

for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
.
This extension is automatically obtained and requires no additional data.

Thus, there is a
discrepancy between the extensions of

\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theories to fs log
schemes.
This discrepancy presents challenges when attempting to
extend a morphism from an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theory to a non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant cohomology theory as the methods for extending these theories are not comparable.
The primary example under consideration is the cyclotomic trace

\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathrm{K}(S)\to\mathrm{TC}(S)

for all
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
.
When
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
,
the cyclotomic trace only gives
\mathrm{Tr}\colon\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\to\mathrm{TC}(X-\partial X)
.
Rognes
[
58
]
conjectured that the
log cyclotomic trace

\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\to\mathrm{logTC}(X)

exists, making the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTC}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{TC}(X-\partial X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}}

commutative.
In this situation,
\mathrm{logTC}(X)
serves as a
better approximation
of
\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)
than
\mathrm{TC}(X-\partial X)
.

An obvious example of
X
where
\mathrm{logTC}(X)
provides a better approximation
than
\mathrm{TC}(X-\partial X)
is
X:=\square_{k}
with a field
k
.
In this case, we have
X-\partial X\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
.
Due to the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance of K-theory for regular noetherian schemes, we have
\mathrm{K}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})\simeq\mathrm{K}(k)
.
However,
\mathrm{TC}(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{1})
is much more complicated than
\mathrm{TC}(k)
.
On the other hand,
\mathrm{logTC}
is
\square
-invariant, which gives us
\mathrm{TC}(\square_{k})\simeq\mathrm{TC}(k)
.

1.3.
Suslin functors in non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant motivic homotopy theories

The purpose of this paper is to offer an alternative method for extending cohomology theories from
\mathrm{Sm}/S
to
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
with
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
ensuring that we obtain
\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)
and
\mathrm{logTC}(X)
for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
when we apply this method to
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{TC}
for
\mathrm{Sm}/S
.

The initial motivation for this method comes from the Suslin functor for the modulus interval

\overline{\Box}
as proposed by Kahn-Miyazaki-Saito-Yamazaki
[
38
, Definition 5.2.5]
.
According to
[
38
, Corollary 6.3.8]
, the Suslin functor makes
\mathbb{Z}(n)[2n]
for every integer
n
into the presheaf

\mathcal{X}=(\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}^{\infty})\mapsto R\Gamma_{\mathrm{mot}}(\overline{\mathcal{X}}-\mathcal{X}^{\infty},\mathbb{Z}(n)[2n])

for modulus pairs
(\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}^{\infty})
over a perfect field
k
,
where
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{mot}}(-,\mathbb{Z}(n))
denotes the weight
n
motivic cohomology complex.
We allow non-smooth
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
for the modulus pair
(\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}^{\infty})
to ensure that the proof does not depend on resolution of singularities.
However, this introduces difficulties in representing cohomology theories.
For instance, we currently know that Hodge cohomology is representable in the modulus setting only in characteristic
0
primarily due to the requirement for resolution of singularities as shown in
[
40
, Theorem 1.3]
.

An analogous result holds in the logarithmic case as well. Binda, Park, and Østvær
[
11
, Theorem 8.2.11]
showed that the Suslin functor for the logarithmic interval

\square:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)
transforms
\mathbb{Z}(n)[2n]
for every integer
n
into the presheaf

X\mapsto R\Gamma_{\mathrm{mot}}(X-\partial X,\mathbb{Z}(n)[2n])

for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/k
over a perfect field
k
admitting resolution of singularities.
In contrast to the modulus case, Hodge cohomology is representable in the
\infty
-category of effective logarithmic motives
\mathrm{logDM}^{\mathrm{eff}}(k)
without relying on resolution of singularities as shown in
[
11
, Theorem 9.7.1]
.

1.4.
Logarithmic motivic homotopy theory over
\mathbb{F}_{1}

The fundamental problem of the Suslin functors for the intervals
\overline{\Box}
and
\square
is that there are too many blow-ups to consider,
which makes the treatment of non-smooth schemes
or the resolution of singularities almost unavoidable.

Our proposal is to work with the field with one element
\mathbb{F}_{1}
as a base,
which would enable us to manage much simpler blow-ups.
There is no general consensus on the definition of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
and the category of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
-schemes, see e.g.
[
44
, §2]
for various approaches.
However, the definition we are using for an
\mathbb{F}_{1}
-scheme is a monoid scheme developed by Kato
[
39
]
and Deitmar
[
24
]
because we want to work with a minimized category of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
-schemes.
In this context, our notation
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
refers to the category of smooth toric monoid schemes,
whose objects are associated with smooth fans, but whose morphisms are more general than morphisms of fans.
Arndt’s thesis
[
5
, §4.4]
suggested the construction of
\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
using monoid schemes.

To develop logarithmic motivic homotopy theory over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
we introduce log monoid schemes in Appendix
C
by adding log structures to monoid schemes.
We can also define
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
in analogy to
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
with
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
.
A morphism
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is called an admissible blow-up if
\underline{f}
is proper birational and
f-\partial f\colon Y-\partial Y\to X-\partial X
is an isomorphism.
Let
\mathrm{Adm}
denote the class of admissible blow-ups in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
In this context,

\square:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\infty)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
serves an interval object of
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}]
, see Proposition
3.10
.
In Definitions
C.16
and
C.23
,
we also have the notions of the Zariski topology and dividing covers in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
This leads us to define the logarithmic motivic homotopy category
\mathrm{logSH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
in Definition
2.9
.
We provide an alternative model

\mathrm{logSH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\simeq\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp}))[(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})^{-1}],

in Theorem
2.16
,
where
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
denotes the set of projections
X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\to X
for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and integer
n\geq 1
.

We also consider the Suslin functor

\mathrm{Sing}^{\square}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],\mathrm{Sp}).

For
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})
and
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
we obtain

\mathrm{Sing}^{\square}L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\mathcal{F}(X):=\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{n\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{Y\in\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{n}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{F}(Y)

in (
3.4
).
Here,
L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],\mathrm{Sp})
denotes the localization functor,
and
\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{n}}
denotes the category of admissible blow-ups of
X\times\square^{n}
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

1.5.
Logarithm functor

To utilize
\mathrm{Sing}^{\square}
defined for log monoid schemes in the original world of log schemes,
consider
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
and
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
having a chart
\mathbb{A}_{P}
for a sharp fs monoid
P
.
Such a sharp chart
P
locally exists by
[
52
, Proposition II.2.3.7]
.
We can then consider the category of admissible blow-ups of
\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\square^{n}\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and we can pull back these admissible blow-ups to
X
.
Inspired by this process,
we construct the
logarithm functor

\mathrm{\ell og}\colon\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Nis}}(\mathrm{Sm}/S,\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{sNis}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/S,\mathrm{Sp})

in Definition
5.25
.
It satisfies

\mathrm{\ell og}\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{n\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{Y_{0}\in\mathrm{Adm}_{\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\square^{n}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{F}(\underline{X}\times_{\underline{\mathbb{A}_{P}}}\underline{Y_{0}})

for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
with a sharp chart
P
, see (
5.11
).

A Nisnevich sheaf of spectra
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathrm{Sm}/S
is called
logarithmic
if

\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\mathrm{\ell og}\mathcal{F}(X\times\square^{n})

for all
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/S
and integers
n\geq 0
.
In this situation,
we show
\mathrm{\ell og}\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)
in Proposition
5.28
,
indicating that
\mathrm{\ell og}\mathcal{F}
is a natural extension of
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
that retains motivic properties.

Our first main theorem asserts that various cohomology theories are logarithmic:

Theorem A

(See Theorems
7.9
,
7.10
,
7.12
, and
7.15
)
.

Let
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the Nisnevich sheaves of spectra

\mathrm{K},\text{ }\mathrm{THH},\text{ }\mathrm{TC},\text{ }R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Zar}}(-,\Omega_{-/S}^{q})

on
\mathrm{Sm}/S
are logarithmic.
Moreover, if
S=\Spec(k)
with a perfect field
k
,
then the Nisnevich sheaf of weight
q
motivic cohomology complexes
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{mot}}(-,\mathbb{Z}(q))
on
\mathrm{Sm}/k
is also logarithmic.

The proof of Theorem
A
relies on Theorem
7.7
,
whose proof makes extensive use of toric geometry and is postponed to
[
55
]
.

As a consequence,
we recover Rognes’ topological Hochschild homology using
\mathrm{\ell og}
:

Theorem B

(See Theorem
8.5
)
.

For every
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
and
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
,
we have a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings in cyclotomic spectra

\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{THH}(X)\simeq\mathrm{logTHH}(X)

and a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{TC}(X)\simeq\mathrm{logTC}(X).

1.6.
Motivic representability of K-theory

For
S\in\mathrm{Rg}
,
we have
\mathrm{log}\mathbf{KGL}\in\mathrm{logSH}(S)
in
[
12
, Definition 6.5.6]
,
which satisfies

\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\simeq\hom_{\mathrm{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma^{2n,n}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathrm{log}\mathbf{KGL})

for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
and integer
n
as stated in
[
12
, Theorem 6.5.7]
.
For general non-regular
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
,
whatever
\mathrm{log}\mathbf{KGL}
is,
it is not the case that such an isomorphism always holds.
Indeed, if
X=\square_{S}
,
then we have the induced commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathrm{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}S_{+},\mathrm{log}\mathbf{KGL})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}(\mathbb{A}_{S}^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hom_{\mathrm{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}(\square_{S})_{+},\mathrm{log}\mathbf{KGL}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\not\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

The left vertical morphism is not an isomorphism in general,
but the right vertical morphism is an isomorphism since
\square
is contractible in
\mathrm{logSH}(S)
.
Hence we cannot have that the two horizontal morphisms are isomorphisms simultaneously.

This is the reason why representing the (non-
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant) K-theory of schemes in
\mathrm{logSH}(S)
has been a nontrivial question.
However,
with Theorem
A
in hand,
we can simply apply
\mathrm{\ell og}
to the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum
\mathbf{KGL}:=(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{K},\cdots)
to obtain the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectrum

\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}\simeq(\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K},\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K},\ldots).

This represents K-theory in the following sense:

Theorem C

(See Theorem
8.13
)
.

For every
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
,
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
, and integer
n
,
there exists a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X)\simeq\hom_{\mathrm{logSH}(S)}(\Sigma^{2n,n}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}).

Note that we have
\mathrm{K}(X)\simeq\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X)
for
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/S
by Theorem
A
.

Together with a motivic version of the Snaith theorem due to Annala-Iwasa
[
3
, Theorem 5.3.3]
,
we have a log motivic version of the Snaith theorem as follows:

Theorem D

(See Theorem
8.15
)
.

For
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
,
there exists a natural isomorphism

\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+}[\beta^{-1}]\simeq\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}

in
\mathrm{logSH}(S)
,
see
Construction
8.14
for
\beta
.

1.7.
Log cyclotomic trace

Let
\mathrm{Rg}
denote the category of regular schemes in
\mathrm{Sch}
.
Let
\mathrm{RglRg}
be the category of “regular log regular fs log schemes,”
i.e., log regular fs log schemes whose underlying schemes are also regular.
Given
Y\in\mathrm{Rg}
with a strict normal crossing divisor
D
,
we can associate
(Y,D)\in\mathrm{RglRg}
whose underlying scheme is
Y
and whose boundary is
D
using the Deligne-Faltings log structure.
We construct the
logarithm functor

\mathrm{\ell og}\colon\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Nis}}(\mathrm{Rg},\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{sNis}}(\mathrm{RglRg},\mathrm{Sp})

in Definition
5.25
.
Combining Theorem
A
and a cdh descent argument in Theorem
6.7
,
we obtain the following result:

Theorem E

(See Theorem
7.11
)
.

For every
X\in\mathrm{RglRg}
,
we have a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X)\simeq\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X).

Another application of Theorem
A
is the construction of the log cyclotomic trace whose existence was conjectured by Rognes
[
58
]
:

Theorem F

(See Theorem
9.1
)
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{RglRg}
or
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
with
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
.
Then there exists a natural morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{logTr}\colon\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X)\to\mathrm{logTC}(X)

that coincides with the usual cyclotomic trace when
X
has the trivial log structure.

By combining the log cyclotomic trace with the comparison between the étale K-theory and TC due to Clausen-Mathew-Morrow in
[
18
, Theorem A]
,
we obtain the following result.
Additional results obtained by similar methods can
be found in Theorems
9.7
,
9.8
, and
9.9
.

Theorem G

(See Theorem
9.10
)
.

Let
R
be a strictly henselian regular local ring with residue characteristic
p>0
,
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be a regular sequence in
R
such that the divisor
(x_{1})+\cdots+(x_{n})
is strict normal crossing.
Then there is a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{K}(R[1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{n}];\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathrm{logTC}((R,(x_{1})+\cdots+(x_{n}));\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

The motivic filtrations on
\mathrm{TC}(A;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
for quasi-syntomic rings
A
are useful for the computation of K-theory.
This includes the recent computation of the homotopy groups of
\mathrm{K}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
due to Antieau-Krause-Nikolaus
[
4
]
.
We hope that the log cyclotomic trace contributes to the computations of the K-theory of schemes when combined with the motivic filtrations on
\mathrm{logTC}((A,M);\mathbb{Z}_{p})
for log quasi-syntomic rings
(A,M)
.
A future project is computing the log syntomic cohomology of
(\mathcal{O}_{K},\langle\pi\rangle)
, which would enhance our understanding of
\mathrm{K}(K;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
Its homotopy groups were previously computed by Rognes-Weibel
[
60
]
for
p=2
and Hesselholt-Madsen
[
36
]
for
p>2
.

Note that Lundemo’s forthcoming paper
[
45
]
also shows that for
X\in\mathrm{RglRg}
,
there is a log cyclotomic trace
\mathrm{K}(X-\partial X)\to\mathrm{logTC}(X)
without claiming that it is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring morphism at least for the case that
\partial X
has
1
irreducible component.
This should be enough for the above project computing the homotopy groups
\pi_{*}\mathrm{K}(K;\mathbb{Z}_{p})
using the log syntomic cohomology of
(\mathcal{O}_{K},\langle\pi\rangle)
.

Nevertheless, our result that the log cyclotomic trace is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring morphism is good to know (e.g., to understand the ring structure on
\pi_{*}\mathrm{K}
via the log cyclotomic trace) and has an application to logarithmic motivic homotopy theory.
We plan to adapt the motivic Atiyah duality due to Annala-Iwasa-Hoyois
[
1
]
to the logarithmic setting in future work.
They used the cyclotomic trace to show that the motivic topological cyclic homology spectrum is a
\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
-module for
S\in\mathrm{Rg}

[
1
, Theorem 1.5]
.
Thanks to the fact that the log cyclotomic trace is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring morphism,
we can regard
\mathrm{log}\mathbf{TC}\in\mathrm{logSH}(S)
in
[
12
, Definition 8.5.3]
as an
\omega^{*}\mathbf{KGL}
-algebra and hence an
\omega^{*}\mathbf{1}
-algebra,
see
[
12
, Construction 4.0.8]
for
\omega^{*}\colon\mathrm{SH}(S)\to\mathrm{logSH}(S)
.
In particular,

\mathrm{log}\mathbf{TC}
is an
\omega^{*}\mathbf{1}
-module.

This would allows us to recover
[
1
, Theorem 1.7]
, which shows that the log crystalline cohomology is independent of the choice of compactifications,
using logarithmic Gysin sequences
[
11
, Theorem 7.5.4]
instead of Tang’s Gysin sequences
[
64
]
.

Notation and conventions

We refer to
[
52
]
for log geometry and
[
21
]
,
[
28
]
, and
[
20
]
for toric geometry.

Every log scheme in the main body is equipped with a Zariski log structure unless otherwise stated.
We employ the following notation throughout the paper.

\mathrm{Sch}

category of finite dimensional noetherian separated schemes

\mathrm{lSch}

category of finite dimensional noetherian separated fs log schemes

\mathrm{Rg}

full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sch}
spanned by regular schemes

\mathrm{lRg}

full subcategory of
\mathrm{lSch}
spanned by log regular schemes

\mathrm{Sm}

class of smooth morphisms in
\mathrm{Sch}

\mathrm{lSm}

class of log smooth morphisms in
\mathrm{lSch}

\mathbf{\Delta}

Simplex category

\mathrm{Spc}

\infty
-category of spaces

\mathrm{Spc}_{*}

\infty
-category of pointed spaces

\mathrm{Sp}

\infty
-category of spectra

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}

hom space in
\mathcal{C}

\hom_{\mathcal{C}}

hom spectra in a stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}

\mathrm{D}(A)

derived
\infty
-category of
A
-modules

\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{V})

\infty
-category of presheaves with values in
\mathcal{V}

\mathrm{Sh}_{t}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{V})

\infty
-category of
t
-sheaves with values in
\mathcal{V}

We regard
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for integers
n\geq 1
as monoid schemes, see Definition
B.24
.
When
X
is a scheme and
Y
is a monoid scheme,
we can take the product
X\times Y
in the unified category of schemes and monoid schemes, see Construction
B.7
.
We have a similar convention when
X
is an fs log scheme and
Y
is an fs log monoid scheme.

For a lattice
L
and its elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
,
let

\mathrm{Cone}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}):=\{x\in L:x=a_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+a_{r}x_{r}\text{ for some $a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}$}\}

be the cone in
L
generated by
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
.

2.
Logarithmic motivic homotopy theory over
\mathbb{F}_{1}

Motivic homotopy theory over a scheme
S
is built out of
\mathrm{Sm}/S
. We cannot classify smooth schemes over
S
Zariski locally unlike algebraic topology, where a manifold is locally an Euclidean space.
This makes motivic homotopy theory over
S
profound but more complicated than algebraic topology.

To avoid complicated algebraic geometry but keep Tate twists for the purpose of deviating from algebraic topology, we work with smooth fans.
See Definition
B.22
for the formal definition of
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Smooth fans are much easier to deal with than smooth schemes because smooth fans are completely determined by combinatorial data.
For example, we have toric resolution of singularities
[
28
, §2.6]
, but resolution of singularities over a field of characteristic
p>0
or mixed characteristic is unknown.

The stable motivic homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
is suggested by Arndt
[
5
, §4.4]
.
In this section,
we define
\mathrm{logSH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
for the purpose of exploiting the simplicity of
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
in logarithmic motivic homotopy theory.
We refer to Appendices
B
and
C
for monoid schemes and log monoid schemes,
which we use throughout this paper.

For a presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and its object
T
,
let
\mathrm{Sp}_{T}(\mathcal{C})
be the formal inversion of
X
in
\mathcal{C}
in the sense of
[
57
, Definition 2.6]
.
By
[
57
, Proposition 2.9]
,
if
\mathcal{D}
is another presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category,
we have the induced functor

(2.1)

\Fun^{\mathrm{L},\otimes}(\mathrm{Sp}_{T}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{D})\to\Fun^{\mathrm{L},\otimes}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),

where
\Fun^{\mathrm{L},\otimes}
denotes the
\infty
-category of colimit preserving symmetric monoidal functors.
Furthermore,
(
2.1
) is fully faithful and its essential image is spanned by the functors
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
such that
F(T)\in\mathcal{D}
is invertible.

Definition 2.1

(cf.
[
5
, §4.4]
)
.

Imitating
[
49
]
,
we define the presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{F}_{1}):=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{*}(\mathbb{F}_{1}):=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc}_{*})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{SH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1}):=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1}):=\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathrm{SH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})).

where
\mathbb{A}^{1}
in the formulation denotes the set of projections
X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to X
for
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and
\mathrm{Zar}
denotes the Zariski topology.

Remark 2.2
.

It is unclear whether
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a symmetric object of
\mathrm{SH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
in the sense of
[
57
, Definition 2.16]
or not. Because this is a condition in
[
57
, Corollary 2.22]
, it is unknown and probably false that there is an equivalence between
\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
and the
\infty
-category of the sequential
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-spectra.
This makes computations like stable motivic homotopy groups of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
complicated.

Remark 2.3
.

As a consequence of
[
70
, Propositions 3.8, 5.9]
and Remark
C.17
,
the following conditions are equivalent for a presheaf
\mathcal{F}
of spaces on
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
:

(1)

For every Zariski distinguished square
Q
,

\mathcal{F}(Q)
is cocartesian.

(2)

\mathcal{F}
is a Zariski sheaf, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
satisfies Čech descent for the Zariski topology.

(3)

\mathcal{F}
is a Zariski hypersheaf, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}
satisfies hyperdescent for the Zariski topology.

We have the same results for presheaves of pointed spaces and presheaves of spectra.

Definition 2.4
.

A morphism
f\colon X\to S
in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is an
admissible blow-up
if
f
is proper and the induced morphism
X-\partial X\to S-\partial S
is an isomorphism.
Observe that such a morphism
f
is birational.
Let
\mathrm{Adm}
be the class of admissible blow-ups in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
or
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Recall from
[
30
, Dual of I.2.2]
that a class of morphisms
\mathcal{A}
in a category
\mathcal{C}
admits a
calculus of right fractions
if the following three conditions are satisfied:

(i)

\mathcal{A}
contains all isomorphisms and closed under compositions.

(ii)

(Right Ore condition) For morphisms
f\colon X\to S
in
\mathcal{C}
and
g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
in
\mathcal{A}
,
there exists a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in
\mathcal{C}
such that
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
.

(iii)

(Right cancellability condition) For morphisms
f,g\colon X\rightrightarrows S
in
\mathcal{C}
and
u\colon S\to S^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
uf=gf
,
there exists
v\colon X^{\prime}\to X
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
fv=gv
.

Proposition 2.5
.

The class
\mathrm{Adm}
in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
) admits a calculus of right fractions.

Proof.

The right cancellability condition is a consequence of Propositions
B.37
and
C.21
.

To check the right Ore condition,
let
f\colon X\to S
and
g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
be morphisms in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
) such that
g
is an admissible blow-up.
We can replace
X
by any admissible blow-up of
X
.
Hence by Proposition
D.9
,
we may also assume
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Consider the normalization
\underline{X^{\prime\prime}}
of the closure of
X-\partial X\simeq(S^{\prime}-\partial S^{\prime})\times_{S-\partial S}(X-\partial X)
in
\underline{S^{\prime}}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{X}
,
see Definition
B.33
for the notion of the closure.
Then Lemma
B.35
shows that
\underline{X^{\prime\prime}}
is a toric monoid scheme in the sense of Definition
B.20
.
By Proposition
D.1
,
there exists a proper birational morphism of monoid schemes
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to\underline{X^{\prime\prime}}
such that
\underline{X^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Consider
X^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
with the underlying scheme
\underline{X^{\prime}}
such that
X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime}:=X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime}
.
Then we have
X^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
by applying Proposition
C.36
to the induced morphism
X^{\prime}\to X
.
To conclude,
observe that the induced morphism
X^{\prime}\to X
is an admissible blow-up.
∎

We refer to Definition
C.23
for dividing covers in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proposition 2.6
.

The class
\mathrm{div}
of dividing covers in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
) admits calculus of right fractions.

Proof.

The right cancellability condition is a consequence of Propositions
B.37
and
C.21
.

To check the right Ore condition,
let
f\colon X\to S
and
g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
be morphisms of log monoid schemes in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
) such that
g
is a dividing cover.
Then the pullback
g^{\prime}\colon X\times_{S}S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
of
g
is a dividing cover in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
by Proposition
C.27
.
Furthermore, there exists a dividing cover
X^{\prime}\to X\times_{S}S^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
by Proposition
D.9
,
and the composite
X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
is a dividing cover by Proposition
C.26
.
∎

Proposition 2.7
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{div}^{-1}]\simeq\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{div}^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}]\simeq\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Propositions
D.9
,
2.5
, and
2.6
.
∎

We refer to
[
11
, Definition 3.1.4]
for the strict Nisnevich topology on
\mathrm{lSch}
,
which we abbreviate as
\mathrm{sNis}
.
Recall that for
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
,

\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
denotes the full subcategory of
\mathrm{lSm}/S
spanned by those
X
such that
\underline{X}
is smooth over
S
.
For
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
,
there exists a strict normal crossing divisor
D
on
\underline{X}
over
S
in the sense of
[
11
, Definition 7.2.1]
such that
X\simeq(\underline{X},D)
by
[
11
, Lemma A.5.10]
,
where
(\underline{X},D)
is equipped with the Deligne-Faltings log structure
[
52
, Definition III.1.7.1]
.

Recall that a dividing cover in
\mathrm{lSch}
is a surjective proper log étale monomorphism.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a dividing cover in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then its pullback
\Spec(\mathbb{Z})\times Y\to\Spec(\mathbb{Z})\times X
is a dividing cover.

Proof.

We can work locally on
X
.
Hence we may assume that
f
is the product of
\mathrm{id}\colon S\to S
and
\mathbb{T}_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
for some
S\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and subdivision
\Delta\to\Sigma
.
Use
[
12
, Example 2.3.10]
to conclude.
∎

Recall from
[
12
]
that for
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
,
we have the presently symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S):=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{sNis}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/S,\mathrm{Sp})[\square^{-1},\mathrm{div}^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{logSH}(S):=\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(S)),

where
\square
in the formulation denotes the set of projections
X\times\square\to X
for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and
\mathrm{div}
in the formulation denotes the set of dividing covers in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
.

Definition 2.9
.

We define the presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1}):=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[\square^{-1},\mathrm{div}^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{logSH}(\mathbb{F}_{1}):=\mathrm{Sp}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})),

where
\square
in the formulation denotes the set of projections
X\times\square\to X
for
X\in\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and
\mathrm{div}
in the formulation denotes the set of dividing covers in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Remark 2.10
.

Like Remark
2.2
,
it is unclear whether
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a symmetric object of
\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
in the sense of
[
57
, Definition 2.16]
or not.

Recall from
[
11
, Definition 7.2.3]
(see also
[
12
, Definition 2.6.1]
) that a proper birational morphism
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
with
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
is an
admissible blow-up along a smooth center
if
X^{\prime}
is identified with the blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{Z}X
in the sense of
[
11
, Definition 7.4.1]
for some smooth subscheme
Z
of
\underline{X}
such that
Z
has strict normal crossing with
\partial X
in the sense of
[
11
, Definition 7.2.1]
and
Z\subset\partial X
.

The categories
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
with
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
are equipped with the cartesian monoidal structures
\times
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category,
and let

M\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/S\to\mathcal{C}

be a
\square
-invariant symmetric monoidal functor sending strict Nisnevich distinguished squares to cartesian squares,
where
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

M
is invariant under dividing covers.

(2)

M
is invariant under admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.

(3)

M
is
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariant for every integer
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
See
[
11
, Theorem 7.2.10]
,
which requires
[
11
, Proposition 7.2.5]
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1) Obvious.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3) See
[
11
, Proposition 7.3.1]
,
which requires
[
11
, Theorem 7.2.10]
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1)
See
[
11
, Theorem 7.7.4]
,
which requires
[
11
, Proposition 7.2.5, Lemma 7.7.1]
.
∎

Definition 2.12
.

A morphism
f\colon X\to S
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is an
admissible blow-up along a smooth center
if
f
is an admissible blow-up and
\underline{f}
is associated with a star subdivision of fans.
Let
\mathrm{SmAdm}
denote the smallest class of morphisms in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
containing all admissible blow-ups along smooth centers and closed under compositions.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category,
and let

M\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}

be a
\square
-invariant symmetric monoidal functor sending Zariski distinguished squares to cartesian squares.

(1)

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Consider
\mathbb{A}^{2}\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and its blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{O}\mathbb{A}^{2}
at the origin, which means the star subdivision of the fan
\mathbb{A}^{2}
relative to itself.
Then the induced morphism

M(X\times[\mathrm{Bl}_{O}\mathbb{A}^{2},\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}])\to M(X\times[\mathbb{A}^{2},\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}])

is an isomorphism if and only if the induced morphism

M(X\times[\mathrm{Bl}_{O}\mathbb{A}^{2},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}])\to M(X\times[\mathbb{A}^{2},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}])

is an isomorphism,
see
Construction
C.18
for the notation
[-,-]
.

(2)

Assume that
M
is invariant under dividing covers.
Then
M
is invariant under admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.

(3)

Assume that
M
is invariant under dividing covers.
Then
M
is
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariant for every integer
n\geq 1
.

(4)

Assume that
M
is
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariant for every integer
n\geq 1
.
Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then the induced morphism

M(X\times[\mathrm{Bl}_{O}\mathbb{A}^{2},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}])\to M(X\times[\mathbb{A}^{2},\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}])

is an isomorphism.

(5)

Assume that
M
is
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariant for every integer
n\geq 1
.
Then
M
is invariant under dividing covers.

Proof.

(1) Argue as in
[
11
, Proposition 7.2.5]
.

(2) Let
f\colon X\to S
be an admissible blow-up along a smooth center in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
We need to show that
M(f)\colon M(X)\to M(S)
is an isomorphism.
This question is Zariski local on
S
.
Hence we may assume
S=\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
for some integers
r,s,t\geq 0
.
To conclude, argue as in
[
11
, Steps 2 and 3 in Theorem 7.2.10]
, but use (1) also.

(3) Argue as in
[
11
, Proposition 7.3.1]
, but use (2) also.

(4) Argue as in
[
11
, Lemma 7.7.1]
, but use (1) also.

(5) Let
f\colon X\to S
be a dividing cover.
We need to show that
M(f)\colon M(X)\to M(S)
is an isomorphism.
This question is Zariski local on
S
.
Hence we may assume
S=\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
for some integers
r,s,t\geq 0
.
Using
[
11
, Lemma 7.7.3]
, we reduce to the case where
f
is induced by a star subdivision of
\mathbb{A}^{r}
relative to a
2
-dimensional cone.
To conclude,
argue as in
[
11
, Theorem 7.7.4]
,
but use (4) also.
∎

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
f\colon X\to S:=\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
be an admissible blow-up in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
with integers
r,s,t\geq 0
.
Then there exists an admissible blow-up
g\colon Y\to X
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
fg
is a composition of admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.

Proof.

The morphism
\underline{f}\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{S}
is associated with a subdivision of fans
\Delta\to\Sigma:=\mathbb{A}^{r+s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
.
Since
f
is an admissible blow-up,
the cone
\sigma:=\mathrm{Cone}(e_{r+1},\ldots,e_{r+s})
is contained in
\Delta
,
where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r+s+t}
are the standard coordinates in the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{r+s+t}
of the fan
\Sigma
.
By Proposition
D.6
, there exists a finite sequence of star subdivisions

\Delta_{m}\to\cdots\to\Delta_{0}:=\Sigma

such that
\Delta_{m}
is a subdivision of
\Delta
and
\sigma\in\Delta_{m}
.
Consider
Y\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
\underline{Y}
is associated with
\Delta_{m}
and
Y-\partial Y\simeq S-\partial S
.
By Proposition
C.36
,
we have
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and note that the induced morphism
Y\to X
satisfies the desired properties.
∎

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category,
and let

M\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathcal{C}

be a
\square
-invariant symmetric monoidal functor sending Zariski distinguished squares to cartesian squares.
Then the following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

M
is invariant under dividing covers.

(2)

M
is invariant under admissible blow-ups along smooth centers.

(3)

M
is invariant under admissible blow-ups.

(4)

M
is
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariant for every integer
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

To show the equivalence between (1), (2), and (4),
translate the references to
[
11
]
used in the proof of Theorem
2.11
in terms of log monoid schemes,
see Proposition
2.13
for the details.
The proofs become simpler for the log monoid scheme case since
[
11
, Construction 7.2.8]
is not needed.

Since (3) implies (2) trivially,
it remains to show that (2) implies (3).
Let
f\colon X\to S
be an admissible blow-up in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
We need to show that
M(f)\colon M(X)\to M(S)
is an isomorphism.
For this, we can work Zariski locally on
S
.
Hence we may assume
S=\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
for some integers
r,s,t\geq 0
.

Consider the category
\mathrm{Adm}_{S}
of admissible blow-ups of
S
,
and consider the smallest class of morphisms
\mathrm{SmAdm}_{S}
in
\mathrm{Adm}_{S}
containing all admissible blow-ups along smooth centers and closed under compositions.
Argue as in Proposition
2.5
and use Lemma
2.14
to show that
\mathrm{SmAdm}_{S}
admits a calculus of right fractions in
\mathrm{Adm}_{S}
.
Using
[
11
, Lemma C.2.1]
(with
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{C}
instead of a
1
-category) and the condition (2),
we see that
M(f)
is an isomorphism.
∎

The next result provides an alternative model of
\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
.

Theorem 2.16
.

There is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\simeq\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})^{-1}],

where
(\mathbb{P}^{\bullet},\mathbb{P}^{\bullet-1})
in the formulation denotes the set of projections
X\times(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\to X
for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and integer
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the implication (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3) in Theorem
2.15
.
∎

For symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
,
let
\Fun^{\otimes}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal functors,
and let
\Fun^{\otimes,L}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
be the
\infty
-category of colimit preserving symmetric monoidal functors.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a pointed presentable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category.
Then the induced functor

\Fun^{\otimes,L}(\mathrm{logSH}(\mathbb{F}_{1}),\mathcal{D})\to\Fun^{\otimes}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathcal{D})

is fully faithful,
and its essential image is spanned by those functors
F\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathcal{D}
satisfying Zariski descent and
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
-invariance for all integers
n\geq 1
such that the cofiber of
F(\mathbb{F}_{1})\xrightarrow{i_{1}}F(\mathbb{P}^{1})
is an invertible object of
\mathcal{D}
,
where
i_{1}
is the
1
-section
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Argue as in
[
57
, Corollary 2.39]
, and use Theorem
2.16
.
∎

Construction 2.18
.

Let
\omega\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}

(resp.
\omega\colon\mathrm{RglRg}\to\mathrm{Rg}
,
resp.
\omega\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/S\to\mathrm{Sm}/S
with
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
)
be the functor sending
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
Y\in\mathrm{RglRg}
, resp.
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
)
to
Y-\partial Y
.
Its left adjoint sends
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
X\in\mathrm{Rg}
, resp.
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/S
) to
X
.
Hence we have the colimit preserving adjoint functors

\displaystyle\omega^{\sharp}:\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc}):\omega_{\sharp}

(resp.
\omega^{\sharp}:\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{Rg},\mathrm{Spc})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{RglRg},\mathrm{Spc}):\omega_{\sharp}
,

resp.
\omega^{\sharp}:\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{Sm}/S,\mathrm{Spc})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/S,\mathrm{Spc}):\omega_{\sharp}
)

such that
\omega^{\sharp}X\simeq X
and
\omega_{\sharp}Y\simeq Y-\partial Y
for
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
X\in\mathrm{Rg}
, resp.
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/S
) and
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
(resp.
Y\in\mathrm{RglRg}
, resp.
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
).

We have similar adjoint functors for
\mathrm{Spc}_{*}
and
\mathrm{Sp}
too.

Construction 2.19
.

The functor
\omega\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and its left adjoint send
\mathbb{A}^{1}
to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
and Zariski distinguished squares to Zariski distinguished squares.
Hence using
[
53
, Corollary 2.1.1]
,
we have the induced adjoint functors

\omega^{\sharp}:\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}]:\omega_{\sharp}

that preserve colimits.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathrm{ver}
be the set of morphisms
f\colon Y\to X
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
f-\partial f\colon Y-\partial Y\to X-\partial X
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 2.21
.

The composite functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\xrightarrow{\omega^{\sharp}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{L_{\mathrm{ver}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Spc})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1},\mathrm{ver}^{-1}]

is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories,
where
L_{\mathrm{ver}}
is the localization functor.
We also have similar results for
\mathrm{H}_{*}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
and
\mathrm{SH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
.

Proof.

We focus on the case of
\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
since the proofs are similar.
Let
\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}
be a right adjoint of
L_{\mathrm{ver}}
,
which is the inclusion functor.
We only need to show that the unit and counit

\mathrm{id}\to\omega_{\sharp}\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}L_{\mathrm{ver}}\omega^{\sharp},\text{ }L_{\mathrm{ver}}\omega^{\sharp}\omega_{\sharp}\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}\to\mathrm{id}

are isomorphisms.

Since
\omega^{\sharp}
and
\omega_{\sharp}
preserve colimits and
Y\simeq\omega_{\sharp}\omega^{\sharp}Y
for every
Y\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
the unit
\mathrm{id}\to\omega_{\sharp}\omega^{\sharp}
is an isomorphism.
Since
\omega\colon\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
sends every morphism in
\mathrm{ver}
to an isomorphism,
we have a natural isomorphism
\omega_{\sharp}\simeq\omega_{\sharp}\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}L_{\mathrm{ver}}
.
It follows that we have
\mathrm{id}\simeq\omega_{\sharp}\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}L_{\mathrm{ver}}\omega^{\sharp}
.

It remains to show
L_{\mathrm{ver}}\omega^{\sharp}\omega_{\sharp}\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}\simeq\mathrm{id}
.
Since
L_{\mathrm{ver}}
is essentially surjective and
\omega_{\sharp}\simeq\omega_{\sharp}\iota_{\mathrm{ver}}L_{\mathrm{ver}}
,
it suffices to show
L_{\mathrm{ver}}\omega^{\sharp}\omega_{\sharp}\simeq L_{\mathrm{ver}}
.
Since
\omega^{\sharp}
,
\omega_{\sharp}
, and
L_{\mathrm{ver}}
preserves colimits,
it suffices to show that the induced morphism
L_{\mathrm{ver}}\omega^{\sharp}\omega_{\sharp}X\to L_{\mathrm{ver}}X
is an isomorphism for
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
This follows from
\omega^{\sharp}\omega_{\sharp}X\simeq X-\partial X
.
∎

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant Zariski sheaf of spectra on
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
If the morphism

p^{*}\colon\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathcal{F}(X\times\square^{n})

induced by the projection
p\colon X\times\square^{n}\to X
is an isomorphism for every
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and integer
n\geq 0
,
then the induced morphism
\mathcal{F}(Y)\to\mathcal{F}(Y-\partial Y)
is an isomorphism for every
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
d:=\max_{y\in Y}\rank\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{Y,y}^{\mathrm{gp}}
.
The question is Zariski local on
Y
,
so we may assume
Y\simeq X\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{d}
with
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
The claim is trivial if
d=0
,
so assume
d>0
.

Consider the Zariski covering
\{\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathbb{A}{N}\}
of
\square
.
Using cartesian products,
we obtain the Zariski covering of
\square^{d}
consisting of
2^{d}
open subschemes.
One of them is
U:=\mathbb{A}{N}^{d}
,
and let
V
be the union of the other open subschemes.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and
\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\mathcal{F}(X\times\square^{n})
,
we have

\mathcal{F}(X\times(U\cup V))\simeq\mathcal{F}(X\times(U\cup V-\partial(U\cup V))).

On the other hand,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(X\times V)\simeq\mathcal{F}(X\times(V-\partial V)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(X\times(U\cap V))\simeq\mathcal{F}(X\times((U\cap V)-\partial(U\cap V)))

by induction.
Combine the three isomorphisms above and use the assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is a Zariski sheaf to conclude.
∎

The following result is analogous to
[
53
, Proposition 2.5.7]
.

Theorem 2.23
.

There is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{SH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\simeq\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}].

Proof.

As in Proposition
2.21
,
we have an equivalence of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{SH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\simeq\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1},\mathrm{ver}^{-1}].

If
\mathcal{F}
is an
(\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathrm{ver})
-invariant Zariski sheaf of spectra on
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
then
\mathcal{F}
is
(\square,\mathrm{div})
-invariant.
If
\mathcal{F}
is
(\mathbb{A}^{1},\square)
-invariant Zariski sheaf of spectra on
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
then
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathrm{ver}
-invariant by Proposition
2.22
.
Hence we have an equivalence of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1},\mathrm{ver}^{-1}]\simeq\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Zar}}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[\square^{-1},\mathrm{div}^{-1},(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}].

To conclude,
observe that the right-hand side is equivalent to
\mathrm{logSH}_{S^{1}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})[(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{-1}]
.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

As in
[
5
, Theorem 3.1.29]
,
we have an isomorphism
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\simeq\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m}
in
\mathrm{H}_{*}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
.
Using the multiplication
\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we have the induced morphism
\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
in
\mathrm{H}_{*}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
,
which yields the morphism
\beta\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
in
\mathrm{H}_{*}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
.
As noted in
[
5
, §1]
,
we can define the
motivic K-theory spectrum
over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{KGL}

\displaystyle:=\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+}[\beta^{-1}]

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+}\xrightarrow{\beta}\Sigma^{-2,-1}\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{P}^{\infty})_{+}\xrightarrow{\beta}\cdots)\in\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1}).

This definition is reasonable since for
S\in\mathrm{Sch}
,
we have
p^{*}\mathbf{KGL}\simeq\mathbf{KGL}
in
\mathrm{SH}(S)
by
[
33
, Theorem 4.17]
or
[
63
, Theorem 1.1]
,
where
p\colon S\to\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1})
is the structure morphism.
Unfortunately, the definition of
\mathbf{KGL}
in
\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
is abstract, and we do not know how to compute the
Snaith K-theory spectrum
of
X
defined by

\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Sna}}(X):=\hom_{\mathrm{SH}(\mathbb{F}_{1})}(\Sigma_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\infty}X_{+},\mathbf{KGL}).

for
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
This behaves differently from the K-theory spectrum
\mathrm{K}(X)
defined in
[
15
, §5.3]
.
For example,
we have
\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Sna}}(\mathbb{P}^{1})\simeq\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Sna}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\oplus\mathrm{K}^{\mathrm{Sna}}(\mathbb{F}_{1})
,
while
\mathrm{K}(\mathbb{F}_{1})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathrm{K}_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1})\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{N}}
by
[
15
, Theorem 5.9, Corollary 5.15]
.

3.
Suslin functor

The Suslin functor for the interval
\mathbb{A}^{1}
makes
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-motivic homotopy theory computationally accessible.
A fundamental example is the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant motivic complex in
[
48
, Definition 3.1]
,
which is obtained by applying the Suslin functor to the sheaf with transfers
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\wedge q})[-q]
for integers
q\geq 0
.

In logarithmic motivic homotopy theory,
the Suslin functor for the interval
\square
plays a similar role. Unlike
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,

\square
is
not
an interval object of
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
since we do not have the multiplication morphism
\square\times\square\to\square
extending the multiplication morphism
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
This is the reason why we need to invert admissible blow-ups in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
to use the Suslin functor for
\square
.
Such an inversion of admissible blow-ups makes the Suslin functor for
\square
more complicated.

The purpose of this section is to review the Suslin functor and its cubical version.
Even though the Suslin and cubical Suslin functors for an interval are equivalent by (
3.3
) in a good case,
the formulation of the cubical Suslin functor can have a computational advantage since it does not use the diagonal morphism of the interval.

Let
\mathrm{ECube}
denote the category considered in
[
38
, Remark B.2.3]
and

[
6
, Définition A.6]
.
Its objects are the the symbols
\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Its morphisms are generated by

\displaystyle\delta_{i,\epsilon}\colon\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n}\to\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n+1}\text{ for }i\in[1,n+1]\text{ and }\epsilon\in[0,1],

\displaystyle p_{i}\colon\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n}\to\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n-1}\text{ for }i\in[1,n],

\displaystyle\mu_{i}\colon\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n}\to\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n-1}\text{ for }i\in[1,n-1].

Let
\mathrm{Cube}
denote the category considered in
[
38
, §B.2.1]
and
[
6
, Définition A.1]
,
which is the subcategory of
\mathrm{ECube}
whose morphisms are only generated by
\delta_{i,\epsilon}
and
p_{i}
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is a category, then a
cubical object of
\mathcal{C}
(resp.
extended cubical object of
\mathcal{C}
) is a functor
\mathrm{Cube}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
(resp.
\mathrm{ECube}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{C}
).

Construction 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a unital monoidal category,
and let
I
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
equipped with morphisms

i_{0},i_{1}\colon\mathbf{1}\rightrightarrows I,\text{ }p\colon I\to\mathbf{1}

satisfying
pi_{0}=pi_{1}=\mathrm{id}
,
where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the unit of
\mathcal{C}
.
We can naturally associate
I^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{Cube}\to\mathcal{C}
as follows.
We have the morphism

\delta_{i,\epsilon}\colon I^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}I^{i-1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\otimes I^{n+1-i}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes i_{\epsilon}\otimes\mathrm{id}}I^{i-1}\otimes I\otimes I^{n+1-i}\xrightarrow{\simeq}I^{n+1}

for integers
n\geq 0
,
1\leq i\leq n+1
, and
\epsilon=0,1
.
We have the
i
th projection

p_{i}\colon I^{n}\to I^{n-1}

for integers
n\geq 0
and
1\leq i\leq n
.

We have the induced functor
\mathbb{Z}[I^{n}]\colon\mathrm{Cube}\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Ab})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We also have
\mathbb{Z}[I^{\bullet}]^{\flat}\in\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
such that

\mathbb{Z}[I^{n}]^{\flat}:=\coker\big(\oplus\delta_{i,0}\colon\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}[I^{n-1}]\to\mathbb{Z}[I^{n}]\big)

in degree
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the differential is induced by
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\delta_{i,1}
,
see also
[
38
, Remark B.1.4]
and
[
6
, Lemma A.3, Remarque A.5]
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a presheaf of complexes on
\mathcal{C}
.
The
cubical Suslin complex

\mathrm{CSing}^{I}\mathcal{F}
in
[
38
, Definition B.6.2]
can be formulated as

\mathrm{CSing}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X):=\hom_{\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))}(\mathbb{Z}[I^{\bullet}]^{\flat}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[X],\mathcal{F})\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})

for
X\in\mathcal{C}
,
where
\hom
denotes the hom complex.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we set

\mathrm{CSing}_{n}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X):=\hom_{\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))}(\mathbb{Z}[I^{n}]^{\flat}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[X],\mathcal{F})\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}).

Example 3.2
.

With the above notation,
assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a presheaf of abelian groups on
\mathcal{C}
.
We have an explicit description of
\mathrm{CSing}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X)
obtained by
[
38
, Lemma B.1.3]
as follows.
In degree
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have

\mathrm{CSing}_{n}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\ker(\mathcal{F}(X\times I^{n})\xrightarrow{\delta_{i,0}^{*}}\mathcal{F}(X\times I^{n-1})),

where we regard
\mathcal{F}(X\times I^{n-1})
as
0
if
n=0
.
The differential is given by

\delta^{*}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\delta_{i,1}^{*}\colon\mathrm{CSing}_{n}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X)\to\mathrm{CSing}_{n-1}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X).

Example 3.3
.

As noted in
[
6
, Remarque A.23]
,
we have the monoidal products on
\mathrm{Cube}
and
\mathrm{ECube}
given by

\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{m}\otimes\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n}:=\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{m+n}

for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Observe that
\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{0}
is the monoidal unit.
Also,
\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{1}
satisfies the condition for
I
in Construction
3.1
.

Construction 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a cubical object of
\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})
.
Then the
cubical geometric realization of
\mathcal{F}
is

\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert_{\mathrm{cube}}:=\hom_{\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{Cube},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))}(\mathbb{Z}[\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{\bullet}]^{\flat},\mathcal{F}).

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a presheaf of complexes on a unital monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
,
and let
I
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
equipped with morphisms
i_{0},i_{1}\colon\mathbf{1}\to I
and
p\colon I\to\mathbf{1}
satisfying
pi_{0}=pi_{1}=\mathrm{id}
,
where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the unit of
\mathcal{C}
.
Then for
X\in\mathcal{C}
,
there is a natural isomorphism of complexes

\mathrm{CSing}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\lvert\mathcal{F}(X\otimes I^{\bullet})\rvert_{\mathrm{cube}}.

Proof.

Consider the functor
\alpha\colon\mathrm{Cube}\to\mathcal{C}
sending
\underline{\mathbf{1}}^{n}
to
X\otimes I^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Use the induced pair of adjunctions

\alpha_{!}:\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{Cube},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})):\alpha^{*}

to compare
\mathrm{CSing}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X)
and
\lvert\mathcal{F}(X\otimes I^{\bullet})\rvert_{\mathrm{cube}}
.
∎

Construction 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a unital monoidal category,
and let
I
be an interval object of
\mathcal{C}
in the sense of
[
38
, Definition B.2.1]
, which is a variant of
[
67
, §2.2]
.
This means that
I
is equipped with morphisms

\mu\colon I\otimes I\to I,\text{ }i_{0},i_{1}\colon\mathbf{1}\rightrightarrows I,\text{ }p\colon I\to\mathbf{1}

such that

(3.1)

pi_{0}=pi_{1}=\mathrm{id},\text{ }\mu(i_{0}\otimes\mathrm{id})=\mu(\mathrm{id}\otimes i_{0})=i_{0}p,\text{ }\mu(i_{1}\otimes\mathrm{id})=\mu(\mathrm{id}\otimes i_{1})=\mathrm{id}.

We can extend
I^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{Cube}\to\mathcal{C}
to
I^{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{ECube}\to\mathcal{C}
by adding the
i
th multiplication

\mu_{i}\colon I^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}I^{i-1}\otimes I^{2}\otimes I^{n-i-1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu\otimes\mathrm{id}}I^{i-1}\otimes I\otimes I^{n-i-1}\xrightarrow{\simeq}I^{n-1}

for integers
n\geq 2
and
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

Construction 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with finite product,
and let
I
be an interval object of
\mathcal{C}
with respect to the monoidal product
\times
.
The functor
\mathrm{Sing}^{I}
in
[
49
, §2.3]
can be formulated in the stable setting as follows.
Consider the cosimplicial object
\Delta_{I}^{\bullet}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\to\mathcal{C}
defined as in
[
49
, p. 88]
.
We have the
Suslin functor

\mathrm{Sing}^{I}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Sp}).

given by

(3.2)

\mathrm{Sing}^{I}\mathcal{F}(X):=\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{n\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{F}(X\times\Delta_{I}^{n})

for
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Sp})
and
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

Consider the adjoint functors

L_{I}:\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Sp})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Sp})[I^{-1}]:\iota_{I},

where
I^{-1}
in the formulation is the set of projections
X\times I\to X
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
,
and
L_{I}
is the localization.
Argue as in
[
48
, Corollary 2.19]
and
[
49
, Corollary 3.8 in §2]
to show that
\mathrm{Sing}^{I}\mathcal{F}
is
I
-local and the induced morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\mathrm{Sing}^{I}\mathcal{F}
is an
I
-local equivalence.
We can also consider
\mathrm{Sing}^{I}
for complexes instead of spectra.

Together with
[
38
, Theorem B.6.3]
,
we have natural isomorphisms

(3.3)

\mathrm{Sing}^{I}\simeq\iota_{I}L_{I}\simeq\mathrm{CSing}^{I}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z})).

Proposition 3.8
.

The monoid scheme
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is an interval object of
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

We have the multiplication morphism
\mu\colon\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
induced by the diagonal map
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\oplus\mathbb{N}
.
Let
i_{0},i_{1}\colon\mathrm{pt}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{A}^{1}
be the
0
-section and
1
-section.
Check (
3.1
) to conclude.
∎

Construction 3.9
.

Let
\mathrm{Bl}_{(\infty,0)+(0,\infty)}(\square^{2})
be the log monoid scheme in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that its underlying monoid scheme is associated with the fan in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
whose maximal cones are

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},e_{2}),\text{ }\mathrm{Cone}(e_{2},e_{2}-e_{1}),\text{ }\mathrm{Cone}(-e_{1},e_{2}-e_{1}),

\displaystyle\text{ }\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},e_{1}-e_{2}),\text{ }\mathrm{Cone}(-e_{2},e_{1}-e_{2}),\text{ }\mathrm{Cone}(-e_{1},-e_{2})

and
\mathrm{Bl}_{(\infty,0)+(0,\infty)}(\square^{2})-\partial\mathrm{Bl}_{(\infty,0)+(0,\infty)}(\square^{2})\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}
.
We have the induced admissible blow-up
p\colon\mathrm{Bl}_{(\infty,0)+(0,\infty)}(\square^{2})\to\square^{2}
.
We also have the morphism

\mu^{\prime}\colon\mathrm{Bl}_{(\infty,0)+(0,\infty)}(\square^{2})\to\square

induced by the summation map of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\mu\colon\square^{2}\to\square
be the morphism
gf^{-1}
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}]
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The log monoid scheme
\square
is an interval object of the category
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}]
.

Proof.

Let
i_{0},i_{1}\colon\mathrm{pt}\rightrightarrows\square^{1}
be the
0
-section and
1
-section.
Consider
\mu
in Construction
3.9
.
Check (
3.1
) to conclude.
∎

Construction 3.11
.

Argue as in
[
56
, Lemma 4.3]
to obtain an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],\mathrm{Sp})\simeq\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}].

We have the Suslin functor

\mathrm{Sing}^{\square}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}],\mathrm{Sp})

and the localization functor

L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}].

Combining all these together,
we obtain the functor

\mathrm{Sing}^{\square}L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}].

For
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{Sp})
,
we have

(3.4)

\mathrm{Sing}^{\square}L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{n\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{Y\in\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{n}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{F}(Y)

by (
3.2
) and Proposition
3.10
,
where
\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{n}}
denotes the category of admissible blow-ups of
X\times\square^{n}
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Similarly, we obtain the functor

\mathrm{CSing}^{\square}L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\colon\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))\to\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))[\mathrm{Adm}^{-1}].

For
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{PSh}(\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1},\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
,
Proposition
3.5
yields

(3.5)

\mathrm{CSing}^{\square}L_{\mathrm{Adm}}\mathcal{F}(X)\simeq\big\lvert\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{Y\in\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{\bullet}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathcal{F}(Y)\big\rvert_{\mathrm{cube}}.

4.
Logarithm functor: Case of monoid schemes

The purpose of this section is to explain how to naturally make a presheaf on
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
into a presheaf on
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
that potentially enjoys motivic properties.

The Suslin functor for
\square
has a slight computational disadvantage: If
X=X^{\prime}\times\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
for some
X^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and a smooth fan
\Sigma
,
then the category
\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{n}}
is unnecessarily more complicated then
\mathrm{Adm}_{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\square^{n}}
even though
\mathrm{Adm}_{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\square^{n}}
contains the essential data.
We refer to Definition
C.20
for
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
.
The idea of the logarithm functor in Definition
4.17
is to work with
\mathrm{Adm}_{\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\square^{n}}
instead of
\mathrm{Adm}_{X\times\square^{n}}
for such an
X
.

We set
\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log}:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},0+\infty)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
For a fan
\Sigma
,
we often regard it as the associated toric monoid scheme.
We also regard the monoid schemes
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for integers
n\geq 1
as the corresponding fans.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
be the category of admissible blow-ups
Y\to X\times\square^{n}
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that there exists a dividing cover
g\colon V\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
satisfying
\underline{f}=\underline{g}
.
Such an admissible blow-up
Y\to X\times\square^{n}
is called a
standard blow-up
.

Observe that every morphism in
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
is an admissible blow-up.
Furthermore, there exists a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\square^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
by Proposition
C.21
,
where
X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}\to X\times\square^{n}
is the canonical map whose underlying morphism of schemes is the identity.

Definition 4.2
.

This is a variant of the above definition,
which is needed for technical convenience. For
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
be the category of dividing covers
V\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
g^{-1}(X\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\to X\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}
is an isomorphism.
Such a dividing cover
V\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
is called a
standard dividing cover.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For
Y,Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
and
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
,

\Hom_{\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}}(Y^{\prime},Y)
and
\Hom_{\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}}(V^{\prime},V)
have at most one element.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Propositions
B.37
and
C.21
.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

For
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and integer
n\geq 0
,
there is a fully faithful functor of categories

\rho\colon\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}\to\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}

such that
\underline{Y}=\underline{\rho(Y)}
for
Y\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
.
Furthermore,

V\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
is in the essential image if and only if
V\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

For
Y\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
, there exists a dividing cover
g\colon V\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
satisfying
\underline{f}=\underline{g}
.
We set
\rho(Y):=V
.
Since
g^{-1}(X\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\simeq X\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}
,
we have
\rho(Y)\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
.
For another
Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
, consider the corresponding dividing cover
V^{\prime}\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
too.
Observe that
\Hom_{\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}}(Y^{\prime},Y)
and
\Hom_{\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}}(V^{\prime},V)
have at most one element by Proposition
4.3
.
If
Y^{\prime}\to Y
is a morphism in
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
,
then we have the composite morphism

V^{\prime}-\partial V^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\simeq}Y^{\prime}\times_{\square^{n}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}\to Y\times_{\square^{n}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}V-\partial V,

which yields a morphism
V^{\prime}\to V
in
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
by Proposition
C.21
.
Conversely,
if
V^{\prime}\to V
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
,
then we have the composite morphism

Y^{\prime}-\partial Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\underline{V^{\prime}}\times_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\to\underline{V}\times_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}}\mathbb{A}^{n}\to Y-\partial Y,

which yields a morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
in
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
by Proposition
C.21
.
It follows that
\rho
has a functor structure and is fully faithful.

Let us identify the essential image of
\rho
.
By definition,
for every
Y\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
,
we have
\rho(Y)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Conversely,
for
V\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
such that
V\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/S
,
consider
Y\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
\underline{Y}:=\underline{V}
and
Y-\partial Y:=(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
.
Then we have an induced admissible blow-up
Y\to X\times\square^{n}
,
so we have
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
by Proposition
C.36
and hence
Y\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
.
Furthermore, we have
\rho(Y)\simeq V
.
∎

Example 4.5
.

Assume
X:=\square^{r}
with an integer
r\geq 0
.
For an integer
n\geq 0
,
consider
(X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n})^{\sharp}
obtained by Construction
C.25
,
which is isomorphic to
(\square-\{0\})^{r}\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
.
A dividing cover
g\colon V\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
corresponds to a subdivision of
(\mathbb{P}^{1}-\{0\})^{r}\times(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}
,
which also corresponds to a subdivision
\Sigma\to(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{r+n}
such that if
a:=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r+n})
is a ray of
\Sigma
such that
a_{i}>0
for some
1\leq i\leq r
, then we have
a=e_{i}
.
The condition

g^{-1}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\simeq(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}

corresponds to the condition
\mathrm{Cone}(e_{r+1},\ldots,e_{r+n})\in\Sigma
.

Next, we discuss some basic properties of
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
and
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then for every
V\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
,
there exists
Z\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
and a morphism
\rho(Z)\to W
in
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
D.1
,
there exists a dividing cover
p\colon W\to V
with
W\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that for every open subscheme
U
of
V
with
U\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
we have
p^{-1}(U)\simeq U
.
In particular,
we have
(gp)^{-1}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\simeq(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}
,
so we have
W\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
.
Since
W\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
we have
W\simeq\rho(Z)
for some
Z\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
by Proposition
4.4
.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then the categories
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
and
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
are cofiltered.

Proof.

Let
V,V^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
.
The fiber product
V^{\prime\prime}:=V\times_{X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}}V^{\prime}
yields
Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
since the preimage of
U:=(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}
in
V^{\prime\prime}
is
U
.
Hence
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
is connected.
Together with Proposition
4.3
,
we see that
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
is cofiltered.

Now let
Y,Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
.
We set
V:=\rho(V)
and
V^{\prime}:=\rho(V^{\prime})
,
and form
V^{\prime\prime}
as above.
By Proposition
4.6
,
there exists a morphism
\rho(Z)\to V^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
with
Z\in\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
.
Then we have morphisms
Z\to Y,Y^{\prime}
by Proposition
4.4
,
so
\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}
is connected and hence cofiltered using Proposition
4.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then the fully faithful functor
\rho\colon\mathrm{SBl}_{X}^{n}\to\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
is cofinal.

Proof.

This is immediate from Propositions
4.6
and
4.7
.
∎

The following two constructions are the key ingredients for establishing the naturality of the functor
\widetilde{\mathrm{\ell og}}
in Definition
4.15
below.

Construction 4.9
.

Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
g\colon V\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
is a dividing cover in
\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}
,
consider its pullback
g^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
.
By Proposition
C.28
,

g^{\prime}
is a dividing cover.
Furthermore,

g^{-1}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\simeq(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}

implies

g^{\prime-1}((X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime})\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\simeq(X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime})\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}

since
f
sends
X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime}
into
X-\partial X
.
Hence we have
V^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X^{\prime}}^{n}
.
The construction
V^{\prime}
is natural,
so we obtain a functor
f^{*}\colon\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{n}\to\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X^{\prime}}^{n}
given by
f^{*}(V):=V^{\prime}
.

Similarly,
let
\alpha\colon[m]\to[m^{\prime}]
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\Delta}
that is one of
p_{i}\colon[n+1]\to[n]
for
0\leq i\leq n
and
\delta_{i}\colon[n]\to[n+1]
for
0\leq i\leq n
but not
i\neq n+1
.
Then we have the induced morphism
(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{\alpha}\colon(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{m^{\prime}}\to(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{m}
such that its underlying morphism
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{m^{\prime}}\to(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{m}
extends
\mathbb{A}^{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{A}^{m^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{A}^{m}
.
Indeed,
this claim is clear for
p_{i}
.
The morphism
(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{\delta_{0}}
is the
1
-section in the first coordinate.
The morphism
(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{\delta_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
is identified with

(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{i-1}\times\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log}\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n-i}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times d\times id}(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{i-1}\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{2}\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n-i},

where
d\colon\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log}\to(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{2}
is the diagonal morphism.
Argue as above to obtain a functor
\alpha^{*}\colon\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{m}\to\mathrm{Sdiv}_{X}^{m^{\prime}}
.

Construction 4.10
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Consider the morphism
\alpha:=\delta_{n}\colon[n-1]\to[n]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
with an integer
n\geq 1
,
and let
g\colon W\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n}
be a partial dividing cover in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
in the sense of Definition
C.23
such that the induced morphism

(4.1)

g^{-1}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n})\to(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}

is an open immersion.
Observe that the morphism
\square^{\alpha}\colon\square^{n-1}\to\square^{n}
is the
0
-section in the
n
th coordinate.

Let
\underline{\alpha^{*}(W)}
be the closure of
(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
in
\underline{W}\times_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}}(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n-1}
,
see Definition
B.33
for the notion of the closure for monoid schemes.
Observe that
\underline{\alpha^{*}(W)}
is a smooth toric monoid scheme by Lemma
B.38
.

We claim that there exists a unique

\alpha^{*}(W)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
with underlying monoid scheme
\underline{\alpha^{*}(W)}
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

\alpha^{*}(W)-\partial\alpha^{*}(W):=((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n-1})\times_{\underline{X}\times(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{n}}\underline{Y}
.

(ii)

Let
\alpha^{*}(g)\colon\alpha^{*}(W)\to X\times(\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log})^{n-1}
be the induced morphism in
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then
\alpha^{*}(g)
is a partial dividing cover.

(iii)

The induced morphism

(4.2)

\alpha^{*}(g)^{-1}((X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n-1})\to(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}{N}^{n-1}

is an open immersion.

The uniqueness is ensured by (i).
We can work Zariski locally on
X
for the claim,
so we may assume
X=\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
for some
r,s,t\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
g
is
\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}\to\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
times a dividing cover.
We can take out the
\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
part,
so we reduce to the case where
s=t=0
.

In this case, let
\alpha^{*}(W):=\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
, where
\Sigma
is the fan associated with
\underline{\alpha^{*}(V)}
.
Then we have (i).
Since
\underline{\alpha^{*}(g)}
is birational,

\alpha^{*}(g)
is a partial dividing cover by Examples
B.32
and
C.24
,
so we have (ii).
Since (
4.1
) is an open immersion.
we have (iii).

Now, consider
U\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
\underline{U}:=\underline{W}
and
U-\partial U:=(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
,
and consider
\alpha^{*}(U)\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that

\underline{\alpha^{*}(U)}:=\underline{\alpha^{*}(V)},\text{ }\alpha^{*}(U)-\partial\alpha^{*}(U):=(X-\partial X)\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}.

By Proposition
C.36
,
we have
U,\alpha^{*}(U)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Furthermore,
we have the induced commutative square
