\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

It suffices to show that the squares in
\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Gys}(Z\to X))
are cartesian for
\mathbb{E}=\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K},\mathrm{logTC}
.
The claim holds for
\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}
by
[
17
, Theorem 13.6.3]
and Theorem
7.11
.
To show the claim for
\mathrm{logTC}
,
it suffices to show that the squares in
\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Gys}(Z\to X))
for
\mathbb{E}=\mathrm{logTHH}
are cocartesian as cyclotomic spectra.
Since the forgetful functor from the
\infty
-category of cyclotomic spectra to the
\infty
-category of spectra is conservative and exact by
[
50
, Corollary II.1.7]
,
it suffices to show that the squares in
\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Gys}(Z\to X))
for
\mathbb{E}=\mathrm{logTHH}
are cocartesian as spectra.

We can work Zariski locally on
Y:=(X,Z)
for this claim.
Hence we may assume that there exists a strict morphism
Y\to Y_{0}:=\mathbb{A}{N}^{n+1}
given by
\Spec(f)
for some map of log rings
f\colon(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}^{n+1}],\mathbb{N}^{n+1})\to(A,M)
.
We set
X_{0}:=\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
Z_{0}:=\mathbb{A}{N}^{n}\times\{0\}
so that
Y_{0}\simeq(X_{0},Z_{0})
.
As in
[
54
, Construction 4.2]
,

\mathrm{Gys}(Z\to X)
is a derived pullback of
\mathrm{Gys}(Z_{0}\to X_{0})
.
Together with Lemma
9.5
,
we see that
\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Gys}(Z\to X))
is a pushout of
\mathbb{E}(\mathrm{Gys}(Z_{0}\to X_{0}))
as
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings.
Hence we reduce to the case of
Z_{0}\to X_{0}
.
In this case, we have
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{Z}
,
so
[
11
, Theorem 7.5.4]
finishes the proof.
∎

Now, we collect several results relating
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{TC}
of log schemes.

Theorem 9.7
.

Let
K
be a local field of residual characteristic
p>0
.
Then there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{K}(K;\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathrm{TC}((\mathcal{O}_{K},\langle\pi\rangle);\mathbb{Z}_{p}),

where
\mathcal{O}_{K}
denotes the ring of integers of
K
, and
\pi
is the uniformizer of
K
.

Proof.

Combine
[
35
, Theorem D]
and Corollary
9.4
.
∎

The following removes the assumption of resolution of singularities in
[
13
, Proposition 7.10]
.
Let
L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}
denote the strict étale hypersheafification functor.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
For
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/k
,
there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

(L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X))_{p}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathrm{logTC}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Proof.

Since the presheaf
\mathrm{logTC}
on
\mathrm{lSch}
is a strict étale hypersheaf by
[
12
, Proposition 8.3.13]
,
we have the induced natural morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{logTr}\colon L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X)\to\mathrm{logTC}(X).

Take the
p
-completions on both sides to obtain

\mathrm{logTr}\colon(L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X))_{p}^{\wedge}\to\mathrm{logTC}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

If
X
has the trivial log structure,
then the claim is due to Geisser-Hesselholt
[
31
, Theorem 4.2.6]
.

For general
X
,
suppose that
Z\in\mathrm{Rg}
is a closed subscheme of
\underline{X}
such that
\partial X+Z
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
\underline{X}
.
Corollary
9.6
yields a morphism of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(Z))_{p}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X))_{p}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X,Z))_{p}^{\wedge}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTC}(Z;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTC}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTC}((X,Z);\mathbb{Z}_{p}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{logTr}}

We finish the proof by induction on the number of irreducible components of
\partial X
.
∎

Theorem 9.9
.

Let
A
be a henselian discrete valuation ring of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
.
For proper
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/A
,
there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

(L_{\mathrm{s\acute{e}t}}^{\wedge}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathrm{K}(X))_{p}^{\wedge}\simeq\mathrm{logTC}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Proof.

If
X
has the trivial log structure,
then this is due to Geisser-Hesselholt
[
32
, Theorem A]
.
Argue as in Theorem
9.8
to conclude.
∎

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
R
be a strictly henselian regular local ring with residue characteristic
p>0
,
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be a regular sequence of
R
such that the divisor
(x_{1})+\cdots+(x_{n})
is strict normal crossing.
Then there is a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

\mathrm{K}(R[1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{n}];\mathbb{Z}_{p})\simeq\mathrm{logTC}(R,(x_{1})+\cdots+(x_{n});\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

Proof.

The case
n=0
without regularity assumption is due to Clausen-Mathew-Morrow
[
32
, Theorem A]
.
Argue as in Theorem
9.8
to conclude.
∎

Construction 9.11
.

Let
S
be the spectrum of a quasi-syntomic ring in the sense of
[
7
, Definition 4.10]
.
Consider the very effective slice filtration
\tilde{\mathrm{f}}_{n}\colon\mathrm{logSH}(S)\to\mathrm{logSH}(S)
with graded pieces
\tilde{\mathrm{s}}_{n}
for integers
n
in
[
13
, Construction 2.4]
and the Bhatt-Morrow-Scholze filtration
\mathrm{Fil}_{n}^{\mathrm{BMS}}\mathrm{log}\mathbf{TC}
in
[
13
, Definition 5.10]
.
By
[
13
, Lemma 2.12, Theorem 5.17]
,
the log cyclotomic trace induces a natural morphism

\tilde{\mathrm{f}}_{n}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}\to\mathrm{Fil}_{n}^{\mathrm{BMS}}\mathrm{log}\mathbf{TC}

in
\mathrm{logSH}(S)
for every integer
n
.
Its graded pieces are

\tilde{\mathrm{s}}_{n}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}\to\mathbf{M}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{syn}}(n)[2n],

see
[
13
, Definition 5.12, Proposition 5.13]
for the right-hand side.
In
[
13
, Theorem 1.4(1)]
, the construction of such a morphism assumed that
S
is the spectrum of a perfect field admitting resolution of singularities,
but now this condition is no longer needed.
Unfortunately, we still need resolution of singularities to identify
\tilde{\mathrm{s}}_{0}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}
with the motivic cohomology spectrum,
i.e., we have not improved
[
56
, Theorem 1.1]
and hence
[
13
, Theorem 3.4]
.

It would be also an interesting question to compare
\tilde{\mathrm{s}}_{0}\mathrm{\ell og}\mathbf{KGL}
with the Elmanto-Morrow motivic cohomology
[
25
]
.

Appendix A
Pointed monoids

A monoid in this paper means a commutative monoid. Let
0
denote the identity of a monoid.
As rings are building blocks for schemes,
pointed monoids are building blocks for monoid schemes.
In this section,
we review the theory of pointed monoids following
[
19
, §1]
.
See also
[
52
, §1]
for the theory of monoids.

Definition A.1
.

A
pointed monoid
is a monoid
A
with a fixed element
\infty
1
1

1

With the multiplicative notation,
0
denotes the base point, and
1
denotes the identity.
,
which we call the
base point of
A
,
such that
x+\infty=\infty
for every
x\in A
.
A
map of pointed monoids
is a map of monoids preserving the base points.

Definition A.2
.

For a monoid
M
,
let
\mathbb{F}_{1}[M]
denote the pointed monoid
M\cup\{\infty\}
with the base point
\infty
such that its monoid operation extends the monoid operation of
M
.
We set
\mathbb{F}_{1}:=\mathbb{F}_{1}[0]
, where
0
means the zero monoid.

Definition A.3
.

A monoid
M
is
integral
if
a+b=a+c
with
a,b,c\in M
implies
b=c
.
In this case,

M
is a submonoid of the group completion
M^{\mathrm{gp}}
.
A pointed monoid
A
is
cancellative
if
a+b=a+c
with
a\in A-\{\infty\}
and
b,c\in A
implies
b=c
.

If
A
is cancellative,
then
A-\{\infty\}
is an integral monoid,
and we have
A\simeq\mathbb{F}_{1}[A-\{\infty\}]
.
On the other hand,
if
M
is an integral monoid,
then
\mathbb{F}_{1}[M]
is cancellative.

Definition A.4
.

A pointed monoid
A
is
torsion free
if
na=nb
with
a,b\in A
and integer
n>0
implies
a=b
.

If
A
is cancellative and
(A-\{\infty\})^{\mathrm{gp}}
is a torsion free abelian group,
then
A
is torsion free.

Definition A.5
.

A monoid
M
is
saturated
if it is integral and satisfies the following condition: For
x\in M^{\mathrm{gp}}
, if there exists an integer
n>0
such that
nx\in M
, then we have
x\in M
.
A pointed monoid
A
is
normal
if it is cancellative and
A-\{\infty\}
is saturated.

For a monoid
M
,
we have the saturation
M^{\mathrm{sat}}

[
52
, Proposition I.1.3.5(1)]
.
For a cancellative monoid
A
,
we have the normalization

(A.1)

A^{\mathrm{nor}}:=\mathbb{F}_{1}[(A-\{\infty\})^{\mathrm{sat}}].

Remark A.6
.

Let
\mathrm{Mon}
be the category of monoids,
and let
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}
be the category of pointed monoids.
Then we have the adjoint functors

\mathbb{F}_{1}[-]\colon\mathrm{Mon}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Mon}_{*}:U,

where
U
is the forgetful functor.

Remark A.7
.

Let
\mathrm{Ring}
be the category of commutative rings.
Then we have the adjoint functors

\mathbb{Z}[-]\colon\mathrm{Mon}\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Ring}:U,

where
U
is the forgetful functor,
and
\mathbb{Z}[M]
denotes the monoid ring for a monoid
M
.
Note that an element of
\mathbb{Z}[M]
is written as a formal sum
\Sigma_{t\in M}a_{t}x^{t}
with
a_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proposition A.8
.

Consider the functor

U\colon\mathrm{Ring}\to\mathrm{Mon}_{*}

forgetting the additive structure and setting
0
in the ring as the base point
\infty
.
This admits a left adjoint sending a pointed monoid
A
to the quotient ring
\mathbb{Z}[A]/(x^{\infty})
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a ring.
We have the natural map

(A.2)

\Hom_{\mathrm{Mon}_{*}}(A,U(R))\to\Hom_{\mathrm{Ring}}(\mathbb{Z}[A]/(x^{\infty}),R)

sending a map
\theta\colon A\to U(R)
to the map
\mathbb{Z}[A]/(x^{\infty})\to R
given by
x^{t}\mapsto\theta(t)
.
We have the natural map

(A.3)

\Hom_{\mathrm{Ring}}(\mathbb{Z}[A]/(x^{\infty}),R)\to\Hom_{\mathrm{Mon}_{*}}(A,U(R))

sending a map
f\colon\mathbb{Z}[A]/(x^{\infty})\to R
to the map
A\to U(R)
given by
t\mapsto f(x^{t})
.
Check that (
A.2
) and (
A.3
) are inverses to each other.
∎

Construction A.9
.

We can unify
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}
and
\mathrm{Ring}
into a single category
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\cup\mathrm{Ring}
,
whose objects are the pointed monoids and rings,
and whose hom sets are given as follows:

\Hom_{\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\cup\mathrm{Ring}}(A,B):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Hom_{\mathrm{Mon}_{*}}(A,B)&\text{if }A,B\in\mathrm{Mon}_{*},\\
\Hom_{\mathrm{Ring}}(A,B)&\text{if }A,B\in\mathrm{Ring},\\
\Hom_{\mathrm{Mon}_{*}}(A,U(B))&\text{if }A\in\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\text{ and }B\in\mathrm{Ring}_{*},\\
\emptyset&\text{if }A\in\mathrm{Ring}\text{ and }B\in\mathrm{Mon}_{*}.\end{array}\right.

Let
\otimes
denote the coproduct in
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\cup\mathrm{Ring}
.
Observe that
\mathbb{F}_{1}
is an initial object of
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\cup\mathrm{Ring}
,
so we have
\otimes=\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}
.

Example A.10
.

By
[
19
, Explanations after Remark 1.7.1]
,
we have the following descriptions of coproducts of pointed monoids.

For pointed monoids
A
and
B
,

A\otimes B
is the smash product,
i.e., the quotient set of
A\times B
such that
(a,\infty)
and
(\infty,b)
are identified with
\infty
for all
a\in A
and
b\in B
.
For maps of pointed monoids
A\to B,C
,
B\otimes_{A}C
is the quotient of
B\otimes C
by the congruence relation generated by
(b+a,c)=(b,a+c)
for
a\in A
,
b\in B
, and
c\in C
.

Example A.11
.

For monoids
M
and
N
,
we have a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{F}_{1}[M]\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{F}_{1}[N]\simeq\mathbb{F}_{1}[M\oplus N],

where
M\oplus N
denotes the coproduct in the category of monoids.

Example A.12
.

For a pointed monoid
A
and a ring
R
,
consider the monoid ring
R[A]
whose elements are written as a formal sum
\Sigma_{t\in A}a_{t}x^{t}
with
a_{t}\in R
.
Using Proposition
A.8
,
one can show

A\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}R\simeq R[A]/(x^{\infty}).

For a monoid
M
,
we also have

\mathbb{F}_{1}[M]\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}R\simeq R[M].

Remark A.13
.

In the category
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\cup\mathrm{Ring}
,
we also have

A\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}B\simeq A\otimes{Z}B.

for
A,B\in\mathrm{Ring}
.
However, this is against the philosophy that
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}
should be neither a ring nor pointed monoid, see e.g.
[
44
, End of §1]
.
Hence we avoid using the notation
A\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}B
.
The reason why we introduce
\mathrm{Mon}_{*}\cup\mathrm{Ring}
is to justify the notation like
R\otimes_{A}B
with
A,B\in\mathrm{Mon}_{*}
and
R\in\mathrm{Ring}
.

Definition A.14
.

An
ideal
I
of a pointed monoid
A
is a subset satisfying the following two conditions:

(i)

\infty\in I
.

(ii)

r\in I
and
a\in A
implies
r+a\in I
.

If
A=\mathbb{F}_{1}[M]
for some monoid
M
,
then there is a one-to-one correspondence between ideals of
M
in the sense of
[
52
, Definition I.1.4.1]
and ideals of
A
given by
(I\subset M)\mapsto(I\cup\{\infty\}\subset A)
.

An ideal
I
of
A
is
prime
if
I\neq A
and
A-I
is closed under
+
.

Definition A.15
.

For a pointed monoid
A
,
let
A^{*}
denote the set of units of
A
,

A map of pointed monoids
A\to B
is
local
if it is local as a map of monoids in the sense of
[
52
, Definition I.4.1.1(1)]
, i.e.,
\theta^{-1}(B^{*})=A^{*}
.

Definition A.16
.

For a pointed monoid
A
and its nonempty subset
S
closed under
+
,
the
localization
A_{S}
is the quotient set of
A\times S
under the following equivalence relation:
(a,s)\sim(a^{\prime},s^{\prime})
if
a+s^{\prime}+s^{\prime\prime}=a^{\prime}+s+s^{\prime\prime}
for some
s^{\prime\prime}\in S
.
Observe that
A_{S}
is a pointed monoid whose monoid operation is the induced one.

For a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
A
,
we set
A_{\mathfrak{p}}:=A_{A-\mathfrak{p}}
.

Appendix B
Monoid schemes

In this section,
we review the theory of monoid schemes following
[
19
]
.

Definition B.1
.

A
pointed monoidal space
2
2

2

This is called
monoid space
in
[
19
, §2]
.
The reason for our choice of the terminology is that we also use the terminology
monoidal spaces
, see Definition
C.1
.

X
is a topological space
X
equipped with a sheaf of pointed monoids
\mathcal{O}_{X}
on
X
.
A
morphism of pointed monoidal spaces

f\colon X\to S
is a morphism of topological spaces
f\colon X\to S
equipped with a morphism of sheaves of pointed monoids
f^{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{O}_{S}\to f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
such that the induced map of the stalks
f^{\sharp}_{x}\colon\mathcal{O}_{S,f(x)}\to\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is local.

Definition B.2
.

For a pointed monoid
A
, the
affine monoid scheme

\Spec(A)
is defined as follows.
The underlying topological space of
\Spec(A)
is the set of primes ideals of
A
endowed with the Zariski topology, which is generated by the base consisting
D(f)
for all
f\in A
with

D(f):=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(A):f\notin\mathfrak{p}\}.

The assignment
D(f)\mapsto A_{f}
defines a presheaf on the base,
and let
\mathcal{O}_{\Spec(A)}
be the associated sheaf on
\Spec(A)
.

Definition B.3
.

A
monoid scheme
is a pointed monoidal space admitting an open covering
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that each
U_{i}
is an affine monoid scheme.

Remark B.4
.

The category of monoid schemes admits fiber products by
[
19
, Proposition 3.1]
. Its proof also shows that fiber products of affine monoid schemes is affine.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
A
be a pointed monoid,
and let
X
be a pointed monoidal space.
Then there is a natural isomorphism

\Hom(X,\Spec(A))\simeq\Hom(A,\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})).

Proof.

Argue as in
[
52
, Proposition II.1.2.2]
.
∎

Definition B.6
.

For a pointed monoid
A
,
we define
\Spec(A)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}:=\Spec(A\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z})
.
For a monoid scheme
X
,
glue this local construction to define
X\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}
.

Construction B.7
.

We can unify the categories of schemes and monoid schemes into a single category, whose objects are the schemes and monoid schemes,
and whose hom sets are given as follows:

(i)

If
S
and
X
are both schemes or both monoid schemes,
then
\Hom(X,S)
is the original one.

(ii)

If
S
is a scheme and
X
is a monoid scheme,
then
\Hom(X,S):=\emptyset
.

(iii)

If
S
is a monoid scheme and
X
is a scheme,
then

\Hom(X,S):=\Hom(X,S\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}).

In this category, the product
\times
agrees with the fiber product
\times_{\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1})}
since
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1})
is a final object.

For a monoid scheme
X
and a ring
R
,
the
R
-realization of
X
is

X_{R}:=X\times_{\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1})}\Spec(R)
.
The local description of
X_{R}
is in Example
A.12
.

Definition B.8
.

A morphism of monoid schemes
j\colon U\to X
is an
open immersion
if
j
is a homeomorphism onto an open subset of
X
and the induced morphism
j^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{U}
is an isomorphism.
Observe that the class of open immersions is closed under compositions and pullbacks.
When
U
is regarded as an open subset of
X
,
we say that
U
is an
open subscheme of
X
.

Definition B.9
.

A morphism of monoid schemes
Z\to X
is a
closed immersion
if for every open affine subscheme
U
of
X
,

Z\times_{X}U
is affine, and the induced map
\Gamma(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})\to\Gamma(Z\times_{X}U,\mathcal{O}_{Z\times_{X}U})
is surjective.

Observe that in this case,
Z\to X
is a homeomorphism onto its image as topological spaces,
which was included as a condition of closed immersions in
[
19
, Definition 2.5]
.
Indeed, we can work locally on
X
for the claim,
and then use
[
19
, Lemma 2.7]
.

When
Z
is regarded as a subset of
X
,
we say that
Z
is a
closed subscheme of
X
.

Proposition B.10
.

The class of closed immersions is closed under compositions and pullbacks.

Proof.

Let
W\to Z\to X
be closed immersions of monoid schemes.
To show that the composite
W\to X
is a closed immersion,
we can work Zariski locally on
X
.
Hence we may assume that
X
is affine.
To conclude,
observe that the composite of surjective maps is surjective.

Similarly,
to show that the class of closed immersions is closed under pullbacks,
it suffices to show that for every surjective map of pointed monoids
A\to B
and a map of pointed monoids
\theta\colon A\to A^{\prime}
,
the pushout
A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}B
is surjective.
For every
(a^{\prime},b)\in A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}B
such that
a^{\prime},b\neq\infty
,
there exists
a\in A
whose image in
B
is
b
.
Then we have
(a^{\prime},b)=(a^{\prime}+\theta(a),0)
by Example
A.10
,
which shows the desired claim.
∎

Definition B.11
.

The
Zariski distinguished square in
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is the set of cartesian squares

(B.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

in
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
U\to X
and
V\to X
are jointly surjective open immersions.
The
Zariski cd-structure on
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is the set of Zariski distinguished squares in
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
The associated topology is the
Zariski topology
.
We refer to
[
70
, Definition 2.1]
for the notion of cd-structures.

Remark B.12
.

Consider a Zariski distinguished square in
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
of the form (
B.1
).
Then the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times_{U}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times_{X}V}

is a Zariski distinguished square,
where the vertical morphisms are the diagonal morphisms.
Furthermore,
any pullback of Zariski distinguished squares in
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is a Zariski distinguished square.
In particular,
the Zariski cd-structure on
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is complete and regular in the sense of
[
70
, Definitions 2.3, 2.10]
by
[
70
, Lemmas 2.5, 2.11]
,
see
[
19
, Theorem 12.7]
.
Furthermore,
it is bounded in the sense of
[
70
, Definition 2.22]
by
[
19
, Theorem 12.8]
.

Remark B.13
.

Let
A
be a pointed monoid.
Then every affine open subscheme of
\Spec(A)
is of the form
\Spec(A_{\mathfrak{p}})
for some prime ideal of
A
by
[
19
, Lemma 2.4]
.
Furthermore,

A_{\mathfrak{p}}=A_{s}
for some element
s\in A
by
[
19
, Lemma 1.3]
.

Definition B.14
.

A monoid scheme
X
is
cancellative
(resp.
normal
,
torsion free
,
locally of finite type
) if the stalk
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is cancellative (resp. normal, torsion free, finitely generated) for every
x\in X
.
This is equivalent to saying that there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
such that
\Gamma(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})
is cancellative (resp. normal, resp. torsion free, resp. finitely generated) for every
x\in X
by Remark
B.13
.

A monoid scheme
X
is
of finite type
if
X
is quasi-compact and locally of finite type.
In this case,
observe that the underlying topological space of
X
has only a finite number of points.

For a cancellative monoid scheme
X
,
we have the
normalization

X_{\mathrm{nor}}
of
X
whose local description is given as (
A.1
).

Definition B.15
.

An integral monoid
M
is
valuative
if
x\in M^{\mathrm{gp}}
implies either
x\in M
or
-x\in M
. As observed in
[
52
, p. 14]
,
every valuative monoid is saturated.

Definition B.16
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of monoid schemes.

(1)

f
is
separated
if the diagonal morphism
X\to X\times_{S}X
is a closed immersion.

(2)

f
satisfies the
valuative criterion of separateness
(resp.
properness
) if for every commutative square of monoid schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[V^{\mathrm{gp}}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[V])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with a valuative monoid
V
,
there exists at most one morphism (resp. a unique morphism) of monoid schemes
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[V])\to X
such that the resulting diagram still commutes.

When
X
and
S
are of finite type,

f
is
proper
if it satisfies the valuative criterion of properness.

Remark B.17
.

Let
k
be a field.
For a morphism of monoid schemes
f\colon X\to S
,

f
satisfies the valuative criterion of separateness (resp. properness) if and only if the
k
-realization
f_{k}\colon X_{k}\to S_{k}
satisfies the valuative criterion of separateness (resp. properness), see
[
19
, Theorem 8.9]
.

Example B.18
.

A closed immersion of monoid schemes satisfies the valuative criterion of properness by
[
19
, Corollary 8.6]
.
For every cancellative monoid scheme of finite type
X
,
the canonical morphism
X^{\mathrm{nor}}\to X
satisfies the valuative criterion of properness by
[
19
, Propositions 6.3, 8.5]
.

Proposition B.19
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of monoid schemes of finite type.
Then
f
satisfies the valuative criterion of separateness if and only if
f
is separated.

Proof.

Use the valuative criterion of separateness for schemes,
[
19
, Proposition 5.13]
, and Remark
B.17
.
∎

Definition B.20
.

A monoid scheme
X
is
toric
if it is connected, separated, normal, torsion free, and of finite type.

Remark B.21
.

For every fan
\Sigma
,
one can naturally associate a monoid scheme by
[
19
, Theorem 4.4]
,
which we often denote by the same notation
\Sigma
when no confusion seems likely to arise.
A monoid scheme is toric if and only if it is associated with a fan by
[
19
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Definition B.22
.

A toric monoid scheme is
smooth
if it is associated with a smooth fan.
Let
\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
denote the category of smooth toric monoid schemes.

Definition B.23
.

Let
M
be a toric monoid, i.e.,
M
is fine saturated and
M^{\mathrm{gp}}
is torsion free.
We use the notation
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[M])
for the fan in the dual lattice
(M^{\mathrm{gp}})^{\vee}
whose single maximal cone is the dual monoid
M^{\vee}
because its associated monoid scheme can be identified with
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[M])
in the sense of Definition
B.2
.

Definition B.24
.

Let
\mathbb{A}^{1}
be the fan
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathbb{N}])
.
Let
\mathbb{G}_{m}
be the fan
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathbb{Z}])
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathbb{P}^{n}
be the fan in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
whose maximal cones are
\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
and

\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\ldots,e_{n},-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{n})

for integers
1\leq i\leq n
,
where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
are the standard coordinates in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Following the convention in Remark
B.21
,
we use the same notations
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and
\mathbb{P}^{n}
for the associated toric monoid schemes.

Remark B.25
.

For a ring
R
and integer
n
,
the schemes
\mathbb{A}_{R}^{1}
,
\mathbb{G}_{m,R}
, and
\mathbb{P}_{R}^{n}
obtained by Construction
B.7
and Definition
B.24
are the usual ones.

Definition B.26
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan in a lattice
N
,
and let
\sigma
be a cone of
\Sigma
.
Consider the projection
\overline{(-)}\colon N\to N/N_{\sigma}
,
where
N_{\sigma}
denotes the sublattice of
N
generated by
\sigma
.
Let
V(\sigma)
(denoted
\mathrm{Star}(\sigma)
in
[
20
, (3.2.8)]
) be the fan in
N/N_{\sigma}
consisting of
\overline{\tau}
for all cones
\tau
of
\Sigma
containing
\sigma
.

Construction B.27
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan with a cone
\tau
.
In
[
28
, p. 53]
,
a natural morphism of schemes
V(\tau){C}\to\Sigma_{\mathbb{C}}
was constructed.
Let us promote this to a natural morphism of monoid scheme
V(\tau)\to\Sigma
as follows.

If
\sigma
is a cone of
\Sigma
containing
\tau
,
then we have the map

\mathbb{F}_{1}[\sigma^{\vee}]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[\sigma^{\vee}\cap\tau^{\bot}]

sending
x
to
x
if
x\in\sigma^{\vee}\cap\tau^{\bot}
and to
\infty
otherwise,
where
\sigma^{\vee}
denotes the dual cone of
\sigma
,
and
\tau^{\bot}
denotes set of
y
in the dual lattice of
\Sigma
such that
\langle x,y\rangle=0
for all
x\in\tau
.
If
\sigma^{\prime}
is a cone of
\Sigma
containing
\sigma
,
then the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{1}[\sigma^{\prime\vee}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{1}[\sigma^{\prime\vee}\cap\tau^{\bot}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{1}[\sigma^{\vee}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{1}[\sigma^{\vee}\cap\tau^{\bot}]}

commutes.
Hence we can glue these maps to construct a natural morphism
V(\tau)\to\Sigma
.

Observe that the morphism of monoid schemes
V(\tau)\to\Sigma
is
not
a morphism in the category of fans and hence not a morphism in the category of monoschemes in the sense of
[
52
, Definition II.1.2.3]
even though
V(\tau)
and
\Sigma
are fans.
Working with monoid schemes has this advantage.

Definition B.28
.

Let
X
be a monoid scheme.
A
rational point of
X
is a morphism of monoid schemes
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1})\to X
.

Example B.29
.

There are exactly two maps of pointed monoids
\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathbb{N}]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
given by
n\mapsto 0
and
n\mapsto\infty
for
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
Hence
\mathbb{A}^{1}
has exactly two rational points
0
and
1
.

There is exactly one map of pointed monoids
\mathbb{F}_{1}[\mathbb{Z}]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
given by
n\mapsto 0
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence
\mathbb{G}_{m}
has exactly one rational point
1
.
Furthermore,
the obvious open immersion
\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
identifies
1
in
\mathbb{G}_{m}
with
1
in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

Hence
\mathbb{P}^{1}
has exactly three rational points
0
,
1
, and
\infty
.

Definition B.30
.

A morphism of monoid schemes
f\colon X\to S
is
birational
if there exists a dense open subscheme
U
of
S
such that
f^{-1}(U)
is dense in
X
and the induced morphism
f^{-1}(U)\to U
is an isomorphism.

Definition B.31
.

A morphism of fans
f\colon\Delta\to\Sigma
is a
partial subdivision
if
f
induces an isomorphism between the lattices of
\Delta
and
\Sigma
.
A partial subdivision
f
is a
subdivision
if
f
sends the support
\lvert\Delta\rvert
onto the support
\lvert\Sigma\rvert
.

Example B.32
.

A subdivision of fans
\Delta\to\Sigma
is the same as a proper birational morphism of toric monoid schemes under the identification of fans into toric monoid schemes,
see
[
19
, Corollary 10.2]
.
Similarly, a partial subdivision of fans corresponds to a birational morphism of toric monoid schemes by
[
19
, Theorem 4.4(2)]
.

Definition B.33
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of monoid schemes.
The
scheme theoretic image of
f
is the closed subscheme
Z
of
S
factoring
f
that is initial among such closed subschemes.
If
f
is an open immersion,
then
Z
is also called the
closure of
X
in
S
.

For the existence of the scheme theoretic image,
we refer to
[
19
, Proposition 9.3]
,
which also provides the local description as follows:
Suppose
S=\Spec(A)
for some pointed monoid
A
.
Then the scheme theoretic image of
f
is identified with the closed subscheme
\Spec(C)
of
S
,
where
C
is the image of the induced map
A\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

Proposition B.34
.

Let
X
be a cancellative monoid scheme,
and let
U
be its dense open subscheme.
Then the closure of
U
in
X
is
X
.

Proof.

We can work Zariski locally on
X
,
so we may assume that
X=\Spec(A)
for some cancellative pointed monoid
A
.
We can also assume
U=\Spec(A_{s})
for some
s\in A-\{\infty\}
.
To conclude,
observe that the image of
A\to A_{s}
is
A
since
A
is cancellative.
∎

Lemma B.35
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of monoid schemes of finite type,
and let
Z
be the scheme theoretic image of
f
.
If
X
is toric and
S
is separated,
Then
Z
is cancellative, and its normalization
Z^{\mathrm{nor}}
is toric.

Proof.

Let
Z^{\prime}
be a connected component of
Z
such that
f(X)\cap Z^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
Then
f^{-1}(Z^{\prime})
is closed and open in
X
.
Since
X
is connected,
we have
f^{-1}(Z^{\prime})=X
.
Hence
f
factors through
Z^{\prime}
,
so we have
Z=Z^{\prime}
, i.e.,
Z
is connected.
Since
S
is separated and
Z
is a closed subscheme of
S
,

Z
is separated.

It remains to show that
Z
is cancellative, torsion free, and of finite type since this implies that
Z^{\mathrm{nor}}
is torsion free and of finite type and
Z^{\mathrm{nor}}
is already normal.
This question is Zariski local on
S
,
so we may assume
S=\Spec(A)
for some finitely generated pointed monoid
A
.
Then we have
Z=\Spec(C)
,
where
C
is the image of the induced map
A\to\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
We can regard
X
as a fan in a lattice
N
.
Since
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a submonoid of
\Gamma(X,\mathbb{F}_{1}[N])\simeq\mathbb{F}_{1}[N]
,

\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is cancellative and torsion free.
Hence
C
is cancellative and torsion free.
To conclude,
observe that
C
is finitely generated since
A
is finitely generated.
∎

Lemma B.36
.

Let
j\colon U\to X
be an open immersion of monoid schemes,
let
i\colon W\to U
be a closed immersion of monoid schemes,
and let
Z
be the scheme theoretic image of
ji
.
Then the induced morphism
W\to Z
is an open immersion.

Proof.

The question is Zariski local on
U
and
X
,
so we may assume that
X=\Spec(A)
,
U=\Spec(A_{s})
, and
W=\Spec(C)
for some pointed monoids
A
and
C
and element
s\in A
.
Then we have
Z=\Spec(B)
,
where
B
is the image of the induced map
A\to C
.
The induced map
B\to C
is injective.
Let
t
be the image of
s
in
B
.
Since
s
is a unit in
A_{s}
,

t
is a unit in
C
.
It follows that the induced map
B_{t}\to C
is injective too.
Since the map
A_{s}\to C
is surjective,
the map
B_{t}\to C
is surjective too.
Hence the map
B\to C
is a localization,
i.e.,

W\to Z
is an open immersion.
∎

Proposition B.37
.

Let
f,g\colon X\rightrightarrows S
be morphisms of monoid schemes.
Assume that
S
is separated and
X
is cancellative.
If
j\colon U\to X
is a dense open immersion of monoid schemes such that
fj=gj
,
then
f=g
.

Proof.

Form the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\times S,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

where
\Delta
is the diagonal morphism.
Since
S
is separated,

\Delta
is a closed immersion.
Hence
i
is a closed immersion too by Proposition
B.10
.
Since
fj=gj
,
j
factors through
i
.
Proposition
B.34
implies that
i
is an isomorphism.
Hence we have
f=g
.
∎

Lemma B.38
.

Let
\Delta\to\Sigma
be a partial subdivision of
n
-dimensional fans with an integer
n\geq 1
,
and let
a
be a ray of
\Sigma
.
Consider the open immersion
U:=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n-1}\to V(a)
from the maximal torus of
V(a)
.
If there exists a ray
b\in\Delta
mapping to
a
,
then the scheme theoretic image
Z
of
U\times_{\Sigma}\Delta
in
\Delta
is isomorphic to
V(b)
.
If not,
then we have
U\times_{\Sigma}\Delta=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We can work locally on
\Sigma
and
\Delta
.
Hence we may assume that
\Delta\to\Sigma
is given by
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q])\to\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
for some map of toric monoids
P\to Q
.

Assume that there exists no ray
b\in\Delta
mapping to
a
.
The composite
U\to\Sigma
factors through the subfan
\Sigma^{\prime}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n-1}
with a single largest cone
a
.
The fiber product
\Sigma^{\prime}\times_{\Sigma}\Delta
is the torus
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
.
We have
U\times_{\Sigma}\Delta=\emptyset
from
(\{0\}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n-1})\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n-1}}\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}=\emptyset
.

Assume that there exists a ray
b\in\Delta
mapping to
a
.
Then we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma.}

Observe that
V(b)\to V(a)
is a partial subdivision and
U\times_{\Sigma}\Delta\simeq U
.
Consider the face
G:=Q\cap b^{\bot}
of
Q
so that we have
V(b)=\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[G])
.
The morphism
V(b)\to\Delta
is induced by the map
\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[G]
sending
x
to
x
if
x\in Q
and to
\infty
otherwise.
Let
C
be the image of the composite map

\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[G]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[G^{\mathrm{gp}}].

Then
\Spec(C)
is isomorphic to
Z
.
To conclude, observe that we have
C\simeq\mathbb{F}_{1}[G]
.
∎

Appendix C
Log monoid schemes

In this section,
we introduce the notion of log monoid schemes imitating
[
52
]
.
We also explain basic properties of log monoid schemes, which are direct analogs of the corresponding properties of log schemes.

Definition C.1
.

Recall from
[
52
, Definition II.1.1.1]
that a
monoidal space
is a topological space
X
equipped with a sheaf of monoids
\mathcal{M}_{X}
on
X
.
A
morphism of monoidal spaces

f\colon X\to S
is a map of topological spaces
f\colon X\to S
equipped with a morphism of sheaves of pointed monoids
f^{\flat}\colon\mathcal{M}_{S}\to f_{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}
such that the induced map of the stalks
f^{\flat}_{x}\colon\mathcal{M}_{S,f(x)}\to\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
is local.

Definition C.2
.

Let
X
be a monoid scheme.
A
prelog structure on
X
is a morphism of sheaves of monoids
\alpha\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
It is a
log structure on
X
if
\alpha^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*})\to\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}
is an isomorphism.
By
[
52
, Proposition II.1.1.5]
,
the inclusion functor from the category of prelog structures on
X
to the category of log structures on
X
admits a left adjoint

(\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{O}_{X})\mapsto(\mathcal{P}^{\log}\to\mathcal{O}_{X})

for a prelog structure
\mathcal{P}
on
X
.
The
log structure on
X
associated with
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathcal{P}^{\log}
.

The
trivial log structure on
X
is the inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Definition C.3
.

A
log monoid scheme
X
is a monoid scheme
\underline{X}
equipped with a log structure
\mathcal{M}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}:=\mathcal{O}_{\underline{X}}
.
Note that we can regard
X
as a monoidal space (resp. pointed monoidal space) if we forget the structure of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{X}
).

A
morphism of log monoid schemes
f\colon X\to S
is a morphism of monoid schemes equipped with a morphism of sheaves of monoids
f^{\flat}\colon\mathcal{M}_{S}\to f_{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}
such that the induced square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\mathcal{M}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\sharp}}

commutes.
Note that this condition automatically implies that
f^{\flat}_{x}\colon\mathcal{M}_{S,f(x)}\to\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
is local for every point
x\in X
.
Hence
f
induces a morphism of monoidal spaces.

A monoid scheme is regarded as a log monoid scheme with the trivial log structure.

Definition C.4
.

We refer to
[
52
, Definition II.2.1.1]
for the notion of charts for monoidal spaces,
which we can use for log monoid schemes.
A log monoid scheme
X
is
coherent
(resp. fine, resp. fs) if locally on
X
, there exists a chart
P
on
X
such that
P
is finitely generated (resp. fine, resp. fs).

Proposition C.5
.

The category of log monoid schemes admits fiber products.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\to S
be morphisms of log monoid schemes.
As in
[
52
, Proof of Proposition III.2.1.2]
,
the fiber product has the following description:
The underlying monoid scheme
\underline{X}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{Y}
.
The log structure is the coproduct of the log structures pulled back from the log structures on
X
,
Y
, and
S
.
∎

Proposition C.6
.

The inclusion functor from the category of fine (resp. fs) log monoid schemes to coherent log monoid schemes admits a right adjoint
X\mapsto X^{\mathrm{int}}
(resp.
X\mapsto X^{\mathrm{sat}}
).
Furthermore,
the category of fine (resp. fs) log monoid schemes admits fiber products,
which is
(-)^{\mathrm{int}}
(resp.
(-)^{\mathrm{sat}}
) of the fiber product in the category of log monoid schemes.

Proof.

Argue as in
[
52
, Proposition II.2.1.5, Corollary II.2.1.6]
.
∎

Remark C.7
.

Let
X,Y\to S
be morphisms of fs monoid schemes.
Then the fiber products
X\times_{S}^{\mathrm{log}}Y
,
X\times_{S}^{\mathrm{int}}Y
, and
X\times_{S}Y
in the categories of log monoid schemes, fine log monoid schemes, and fs log monoid schemes respectively can be non-isomorphic and hence have to be distinguished.

Definition C.8
.

A morphism of log monoid schemes
f\colon X\to S
is
strict
if the induced morphism of sheaves of monoids
(f^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{S})^{\log}\to\mathcal{M}_{X}
is an isomorphism.

Proposition C.9
.

A morphism of log monoid schemes
f\colon X\to S
is strict if and only if the induced morphism
X\to\underline{X}\times_{\underline{S}}S
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Argue as in
[
52
, Proposition III.1.2.5]
.
∎

Definition C.10
.

A
log monoid
(A,M)
is a pair of a pointed monoid
A
and a monoid
M
equipped with a map of monoids
M\to A
.
A
map of log monoids
is an obvious commutative square.

Let
\Spec(A,M)
be the log monoid scheme whose underlying scheme is
\Spec(A)
and log structure is associated with the constant prelog structure
M
.

Remark C.11
.

Let
X
be a log monoid scheme.
Observe that
X
is coherent (resp. fine, resp. fs) if and only if
X
is locally isomorphic to
\Spec(A,M)
for some log monoid
(A,M)
such that
\overline{M}:=M/M^{*}
is finitely generated (resp. fine, resp. fs).

Definition C.12
.

For a monoid
P
,
we set
\mathbb{A}_{P}:=\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P],P)
.

Example C.13
.

Let
(A,M)
be a log monoid,
and let
A\to B
be a map of pointed monoids.
Then the induced morphism of log monoid schemes
\Spec(B,M)\to\Spec(A,M)
is strict.

Proposition C.14
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of log monoid schemes.
Assume that
X
has the trivial log structure.
Then for every point
x\in X
,
the log structure on
S
at
f(x)
is trivial.

Proof.

Recall that the map
f^{\flat}_{x}\colon\mathcal{M}_{S,f(x)}\to\mathcal{M}_{X,x}
is local,
which means

(f^{\flat}_{x})^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{*})\simeq\mathcal{M}_{S,f(x)}^{*}.

Since
X
has the trivial log structure,
we have
\mathcal{M}_{X,x}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{X,x}^{*}
.
Hence we have
\mathcal{M}_{S,f(x)}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{S,f(x)}^{*}
, which means that the log structure on
S
at
f(x)
is trivial.
∎

Definition C.15
.

Let
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
be the full subcategory of the category of log monoid schemes
X
satisfying the following condition:
The underlying monoid scheme
\underline{X}
is toric, and Zariski locally on
\underline{X}
,
X
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some toric monoid
P
and integer
m\geq 0
.

Let
\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
spanned by those
X
such that
\underline{X}
is a smooth toric monoid scheme.
Observe that Zariski locally on
\underline{X}
,
X
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
for some integers
r,s,t\geq 0
.

Definition C.16
.

A
Zariski distinguished square in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is a cartesian square

(C.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
U\to X
and
V\to X
are jointly surjective open immersions.
The
Zariski cd-structure on
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is the set of Zariski distinguished squares in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
The associated topology is the
Zariski topology
.

Remark C.17
.

The Zariski cd-structure on
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
satisfies the claims analogous to Remark
B.12
.
In particular,
the Zariski cd-structure on
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is bounded, complete, and regular.

Construction C.18
.

Let
S
be a smooth toric monoid scheme associated with a smooth fan
\Sigma
,
and let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
be different rays of
\Sigma
.
We define the log monoid scheme
X:=(S,Z)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
with the notation
Z:=V(\alpha_{1})+\cdots+V(\alpha_{n})
as follows.

Let
U:=X-\partial X
be the open subscheme of
S
associated with the subfan of
\Sigma
consisting of the cones
\sigma
such that
\sigma
does not contain
\alpha_{i}
for every
i
.
Assume
S=\mathbb{A}^{r}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
and
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq d
with integers
d\leq r
and
t\geq 0
,
where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r+t}
are the standard coordinates in
\mathbb{Z}^{r+t}
.
Then we have
U\simeq\mathbb{A}{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{A}^{r-d}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
.
Hence with
P:=\mathbb{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}^{t}
and
F:=\mathbb{N}^{r-d}\times 0\times\mathbb{Z}^{t}
, we have
X\simeq\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
and
U\simeq\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P_{F}])
.
In this case, we define
(S,Z):=\Spec(F\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
.
Glue this local construction to define
(S,Z)
for general
X
.

Observe that for
x\in X
,
X
has the trivial log structure at
x
if and only if
x\in X-\partial X
.

Definition C.19
.

In the fan
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with an integer
n\geq 1

consider the ray
\alpha:=(-1,\ldots,-1)
, and we regard
V(\alpha)
as the hyperplane
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
at
\infty
.
Then we have
(\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}^{n-1})\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
We set
\square:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{0})
.

In the fan
\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
consider the rays
e_{1}
and
-e_{1}
,
and we regard
V(e_{1})
as the point
0
and
V(-e_{1})
as the point
\infty
.
Then we have
\mathbb{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\log}:=(\mathbb{P}^{1},0+\infty)\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Definition C.20
.

For an
n
-dimensional fan
\Sigma
,
let
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
be the log monoid scheme in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
defined as follows.
If
\Sigma=\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
for some toric monoid
P
,
then
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}:=\mathbb{A}_{P}
.
For general
P
, glue this local construction to define
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
.
Observe that
\Sigma
is a smooth fan if and only if
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proposition C.21
.

Let
S,X\in\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then we have

\Hom(X,S)\simeq\{\underline{f}\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{S}:\underline{f}(X-\partial X)\subset S-\partial S\}.

Proof.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then its underlying morphism of schemes is
\underline{f}\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{S}
,
and this sends
X-\partial X
into
S-\partial S
by Proposition
C.14
.

Conversely,
let
\underline{f}\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{S}
be a morphism of monoid schemes such that
\underline{f}(X-\partial X)\subset S-\partial S
.
Assume that
\underline{f}
is associated with a map of monoids
\mathbb{F}_{1}[P]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q]
for some toric monoids
P
and
Q
and
X-\partial X
and
S-\partial S
are associated with
\mathbb{F}_{1}[P_{F}]
and
\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q_{G}]
for some faces
F
and
G
of
P
and
Q
.
Then the condition
f(X-\partial X)\subset S-\partial S
implies that the composite map
\mathbb{F}_{1}[P]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q_{G}]
factors through the localization
\mathbb{F}_{1}[P]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[P_{F}]
.
Hence
\mathbb{F}_{1}[P]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q]
sends
F
into
G
,
so we have a map of log monoids
(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P],F)\to(\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q],G)
.
For general
\underline{f}
,
glue this local construction to obtain a morphism
X\to S
.

To conclude,
observe that the above two constructions are inverses to each other.
∎

Example C.22
.

Let
X
be a monoid scheme,
and let
P
be a monoid.
As in
[
52
, Proposition III.1.2.4]
,
there is a natural isomorphism

\Hom(X,\mathbb{A}_{P})\simeq\Hom(P,\Gamma(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})).

Definition C.23
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
\underline{f}\colon\underline{X}\to\underline{S}
is proper birational.
We say that
f
is a
dividing cover
(resp.
partial dividing cover
) if Zariski locally on
S
, it is of the form
\mathbb{T}_{u}\times\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}\to\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some toric monoid
P
,
subdivision (resp. partial subdivision) of fans
u\colon\Sigma\to\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
, and integer
m\geq 0
.

Example C.24
.

For a subdivision (resp. partial subdivision) of fans
\Delta\to\Sigma
,
the induced morphism
\mathbb{T}_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
is a dividing (resp. partial dividing) cover.

Construction C.25
.

Let
S\in\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Consider the fans
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
whose associated monoid schemes are
\underline{S}
and
S-\partial S
.
Let
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
be the subfan of
\Sigma
consisting of those cones
\sigma
such that all the rays of
\sigma
are not in
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
We set
S^{\sharp}:=\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}
,
which we regard as an open subscheme of
S
.

In the local case
S=\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
with a toric monoid
P
and integer
m\geq 0
,

\Sigma
is
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
,

\Sigma^{\prime}
is
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P^{\mathrm{gp}}])\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
,
and
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
is
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{m}
.
Hence the categories of dividing covers of
S
and
S^{\sharp}
are equivalent.

For general
S
,
we can glue the local constructions to have the same conclusion.

Proposition C.26
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a dividing cover in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
If
S=\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some fan
\Sigma
and integer
m\geq 0
,
then
f
can be identified with
\mathbb{T}_{u}\times\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\Delta}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some subdivision
u\colon\Delta\to\Sigma
.

Proof.

If
\Sigma=\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
for some toric monoid
P
,
then the claim is due to the definition of dividing covers since every Zariski covering of
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
contains
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
itself.
For general
\Sigma
,
glue the local constructions.
∎

Proposition C.27
.

The class of dividing covers in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is closed under compositions.

Proof.

Let
f\colon X\to S
and
g\colon Y\to X
be dividing covers in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
To show that
fg
is a dividing cover,
we can work Zariski locally on
S
.
Hence we may assume that
f=\mathbb{T}_{u}\times\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}\to\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some subdivision of fans
u\colon\Sigma\to\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
with a toric monoid
P
and integer
m\geq 0
.
By Proposition
C.26
,

g
is identified with
\mathbb{T}_{v}\times\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\Delta}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some subdivision of fans
v\colon\Delta\to\Sigma
.
The composite morphism
gf
is identified with
\mathbb{T}_{uv}\times\mathrm{id}
, so it is a dividing cover.
∎

Proposition C.28
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a dividing cover in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and let
g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
be a morphism in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then the pullback
X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
of
f
in the category of fs log monoid schemes is a dividing cover in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Furthermore,

\underline{X^{\prime}}
is the normalization of the closure of
X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime}
in
\underline{X}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{S^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We can work Zariski locally on
S
,
so we may assume that
f
is of the form
T_{u}\times\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}\to\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some subdivision of fans
u\colon\Sigma\to\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
and integer
m\geq 0
.
Since
T_{u}\times\mathrm{id}
is a pullback of
T_{u}
,
we reduce to the case of
m=0
.

We can work Zariski locally on
S^{\prime}
too, so we may assume
S^{\prime}=\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{A}^{m^{\prime}}
for some toric monoid
P^{\prime}
and integer
m^{\prime}\geq 0
.
We have
\Gamma(S^{\prime},\mathcal{M}_{S^{\prime}})\simeq\Gamma(\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}}})\simeq P^{\prime}
,
so the morphism
S^{\prime}\to S
factors through the projection
S^{\prime}\to\mathbb{A}_{P^{\prime}}
by Example
C.22
.
Hence we also reduce to the case of
m^{\prime}=0
.
In this case,

g=\mathbb{A}_{\theta}
for some map of monoids
\theta\colon P\to P^{\prime}
.

Let
\{\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q_{i}])\}_{i\in I}
be the cones of
\Sigma
,
and let
Q_{i}^{\prime}
be the coproduct
Q\oplus_{P}^{\mathrm{mon}}P^{\prime}
in the category of monoids,
and consider its saturation
(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{sat}}
.
Then the cones
\{\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{sat}}])\}_{i\in I}
form a fan
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
and the morphism
X^{\prime}\to X
can be identified with
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
with a subdivision
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
.
Hence
X^{\prime}\to X
is a dividing cover.

For the last claim,
consider
X_{i}:=\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q_{i}])
and
X_{i}^{\prime}=\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{sat}}])
for
i\in I
.
Then we have
\underline{X_{i}}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{S^{\prime}}\simeq\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q_{i}^{\prime}])
and
X_{i}^{\prime}-\partial X_{i}^{\prime}\simeq\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q^{\mathrm{gp}}\oplus_{P^{\mathrm{gp}}}P^{\prime\mathrm{gp}}])
.
Also, the image of the induced map
\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q_{i}^{\prime}]\to\mathbb{F}_{1}[Q^{\mathrm{gp}}\oplus_{P^{\mathrm{gp}}}P^{\prime\mathrm{gp}}]
is precisely
\mathbb{F}_{1}[(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{int}}]
.
Hence the integral closure of
X^{\prime}-\partial X^{\prime}
in
\underline{X}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{S^{\prime}}
is
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{int}}])
using the local description of the scheme theoretic image in Definition
B.33
.
To conclude,
observe that the normalization of
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{int}}])
is
\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[(Q_{i}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{sat}}])
.
∎

Proposition C.29
.

A partial dividing cover
f\colon X\to S
in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is a monomorphism of fs log monoid schemes.

Proof.

We can work Zariski locally on
S
,
so we may assume that
f
is given by
\mathbb{T}_{u}\times\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}\to\mathbb{A}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{m}
for some toric monoid
P
, partial subdivision of fans
u\colon\Sigma\to\Spec(\mathbb{F}_{1}[P])
, and integer
m\geq 0
.
We further reduce to the case of
m=0
since any pullback of a monomorphism is a monomorphism.

We need to show that the diagonal morphism
X\to X\times_{S}X
is an isomorphism.
Let
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
be cones of
\Sigma
,
and let
U_{\sigma}
be the corresponding open subscheme of
\underline{X}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
C.28
,

\underline{X\times_{S}X}
is the normalization of the closure of
X-\partial X
in
\underline{X}\times_{\underline{S}}\underline{X}
.
Using this,
we can show

U_{\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}}\simeq U_{\sigma}\times_{S}U_{\sigma^{\prime}}.

Glue these local constructions to obtain
X\simeq X\times_{S}X
.
∎

Definition C.30
.

For a log monoid
(A,M)
,
we define
\Spec(A,M)\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}:=\Spec(A\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z},M)
.
For a coherent log monoid scheme
X
,
glue the local constructions to define
X\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}
.

Construction C.31
.

As in Construction
B.7
,
we can unify the categories of coherent log schemes and coherent log monoid schemes into a single category whose hom sets are given as follows:

(i)

If
S
and
X
are both coherent log schemes or coherent log monoid schemes,
then
\Hom(X,S)
is the original one.

(ii)

If
S
is a coherent log scheme and
X
is a coherent log monoid scheme,
then

\Hom(X,S):=\emptyset.

(iii)

If
S
is a coherent log monoid scheme and
X
is a coherent log scheme,
then

\Hom(X,S):=\Hom(X,S\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}).

Definition C.32
.

Let
X
be an fs log scheme,
and let
X_{0}
be an fs log monoid scheme.
A morphism
X\to X_{0}
is
strict
if the induce morphism
X\to\underline{X}\times_{\underline{X_{0}}}X_{0}
is an isomorphism.

Definition C.33
.

Let
\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
be the category of pairs
X:=[\underline{X},X-\partial X]
equipped with an open immersion of toric monoid schemes
X-\partial X\to\underline{X}
such that
X-\partial X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

We also have the Zariski topology on
\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
which consists of the families
\{U_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
with finite
I
such that
\{\underline{U_{i}}\to\underline{X}\}_{i\in I}
is the Zariski covering of monoid schemes.

A morphism
X\to S
in
\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
is an
admissible blow-up
if
\underline{X}\to\underline{S}
is proper and the induced morphism
X-\partial X\to S-\partial S
is an isomorphism.

Remark C.34
.

We have the functor
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
sending
X
to
[\underline{X},X-\partial X]
,
which is fully faithful by Proposition
C.21
.

Proposition C.35
.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth fan,
and let
\Delta
be its subfan.
Regard them as smooth toric monoid schemes.
Then we have
[\Sigma,\Delta]\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
if and only if for every cone
\sigma\in\Sigma
not in
\Delta
,
there exists a ray of
\sigma
not in
\Delta
.

Proof.

Assume that for every cone
\sigma\in\Sigma
not in
\Delta
,
there exists a ray of
\sigma
not in
\Delta
.
Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}
be the set of rays of
\Sigma
not in
\Delta
.
Then
\Delta
is precisely the set of cones
\delta
of
\Sigma
such that all the rays of
\delta
are different from
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}
.
By construction
C.18
,
we have
[\Sigma,\Delta]\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Conversely,
assume
[\Sigma,\Delta]\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
We can work Zariski locally on
\Sigma
,
so we may assume
[\Sigma,\Delta]=\mathbb{A}{N}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{s}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}^{t}
for some integers
r,s,t\geq 0
.
The desired condition holds for this case.
∎

Proposition C.36
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a birational morphism in
\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that the induced morphism
f^{-1}(S-\partial S)\to(S-\partial S)
is an open immersion.
If
\underline{X}\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
S\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
then we have
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

We have
S=[\Sigma,\Delta]
and
X=[\Sigma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}]
for a partial subdivision of smooth fans
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
and subfans
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
The assumption on
f
implies that
\Delta^{\prime}
is a subfan of
\Delta
and
\lvert\Delta^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\Sigma^{\prime}\rvert\cap\lvert\Delta\rvert
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be a cone of
\Sigma^{\prime}
not in
\Delta^{\prime}
such that every ray of
\sigma
is in
\Delta^{\prime}
.
By Proposition
C.35
,
we only need to show that this leads to a contradiction.

We have
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathrm{Cone}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
for some rays
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in\Delta^{\prime}
.
Let
\sigma
be the smallest cone of
\Sigma
containing
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Then we have
\dim\sigma=\dim\sigma^{\prime}=d
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in\Sigma
,
so we have
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
.
By Proposition
C.35
,
we have
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Delta
since
S\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and hence
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
since
\lvert\Delta^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\Sigma^{\prime}\rvert\cap\lvert\Delta\rvert
,
which is a contradiction.
∎

Appendix D
Toric resolution of singularities

In this section,
we collect several results on toric resolution of singularities that are needed in this paper.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a fan.
Then there exists a subdivision of fans
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

\Sigma^{\prime}
is smooth.

(ii)

If
\sigma
is a smooth cone of
\Sigma
,
then
\sigma
is a cone of
\Sigma^{\prime}
,
i.e.,
\sigma
is not divided in
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

(iii)

\Sigma^{\prime}
is obtained by a finite sequence of star subdivisions.

Proof.

See
[
20
, Theorem 11.1.9]
.
∎

Proposition D.2
.

Let
X
be a toric monoid scheme.
Then there exists a proper birational morphism of monoid schemes
Y\to X
such that
Y\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Proposition
D.1
due to the correspondence between toric monoid schemes and fans.
∎

Definition D.3
.

Let
\sigma
and
\tau
be cones in a lattice
N
.
We say that
\tau
crosses
\sigma
if
\sigma\cap\tau
is not a face of
\sigma
.
Let
\Sigma
be a fan in
N
.
We say that
\tau
crosses
\Sigma
if there exists
\sigma\in\Sigma
such that
\tau
crosses
\sigma
.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
\sigma
and
\tau
be
n
-dimensional cones in an
n
-dimensional lattice
N
with an integer
n\geq 0
.
If
\tau
crosses
\sigma
,
then there exists an
(n-1)
-dimensional face of
\tau
crossing
\sigma
.

Proof.

Since
\sigma\cap\tau
is not a face of
\sigma
,
there exists a face
\eta
of
\sigma\cap\tau
such that
\dim\eta\leq n-1
and
\eta
is not a face of
\sigma
.
Let
\eta^{\prime}
be an
(n-1)
-dimensional face of
\tau
containing
\eta
.
Then
\eta^{\prime}
crosses
\sigma
.
∎

Lemma D.5
.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth fan in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with the support
\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
where
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
be the standard coordinates in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
If
\eta:=\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r})
with an integer
0\leq r\leq n
and
\tau
is an
(n-1)
-dimensional cone contained in
\mathbb{N}^{n}
such that
\eta\cap\tau
is a face of
\eta
,
then there is a subdivision
\Sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
obtained by a sequence of star subdivisions relative to
2
-dimensional cones such that
\eta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\tau
does not cross
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality,
we may assume
\eta\cap\tau=\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{s})
with an integer
0\leq s\leq r
.
We proceed by induction on
n
.
The claim is obvious if
n=0
.
Assume
n\geq 1
.

We first treat the case where
\tau
is the intersection of a hyperplane and
\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
i.e.,

\tau=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:a_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}x_{n}=0\}

for some
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}-0
.
Observe that we have
a_{1}=\cdots=a_{s}=0
and
a_{s+1},\ldots,a_{r}\neq 0
.
Since
\eta\cap\tau
is a face of
\eta
,

a_{s+1},\ldots,a_{r}
have the same sign.
We may assume that they are positive.
Consider the function
\varphi_{\tau}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
given by

\varphi_{\tau}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}):=a_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}x_{n}.

According to
[
22
, Proof of Lemma 2.3]
,
there exists a sequence of star subdivisions
\Sigma^{\prime}:=\Sigma_{m}\to\cdots\to\Sigma_{0}:=\Sigma
such that
\Sigma^{\prime}
does not cross
\tau
and
\Sigma_{i+1}
is the star subdivision of
\Sigma_{i}
relative to a
2
-dimensional cone
\delta_{i}
for each
i
satisfying the following condition:
One vertex
v
of
\delta_{i}
satisfies
\varphi_{\tau}(v)>0
and the other vertex
v^{\prime}
of
\delta_{i}
satisfies
\varphi_{\tau}(v^{\prime})<0
.
Since
\varphi_{\tau}(e_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{\tau}(e_{r})\geq 0
,
the center of
\delta_{i}
is not in
\eta
.
Hence we have
\eta\in\Sigma_{i}
for each
i
by induction,
so
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a desired one.

For general
\tau
,
consider

\delta:=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:-x_{s+1}-\cdots-x_{r}+mx_{r+1}+\cdots+mx_{n}=0\}

with an integer
m\geq 0
.
If
f
is a ray of
\tau
different from
e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}
,
then
f\notin\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r})
since
\eta\cap\tau=\mathrm{Cone}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{s})
.
Hence there exists an integer
m>0
such that
\varphi_{\delta}(f)>0
for such a ray
f
.
By the special case above,
there exists a subdivision
\Delta
of
\Sigma
containing
\eta
and obtained by a sequence of star subdivisions relative to
2
-dimensional cones such that if
\delta
does not cross
\Delta
.
Then every cone of
\Delta
is contained either in the region
\varphi_{\delta}\geq 0
or
\varphi_{\delta}\leq 0
.
Let
\Delta^{\prime}
be the subfan of
\Delta
consisting of the cones in the region
\varphi_{\delta}\geq 0
.
Then we have
\tau\subset\lvert\Delta^{\prime}\rvert
.
Hence we can replace
\Sigma
by
\Delta^{\prime}
,
so we may assume
\eta\cap\tau=\eta
.

With this assumption,
let
\overline{\tau}
be the intersection of
\mathbb{N}^{n}
and the hyperplane generated by
\tau
.
We have
\overline{\tau}\cap\eta=\eta
since
\tau\cap\eta=\eta
.
By the special case above,
there exists a subdivision
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
obtained by a finite sequence of star subdivisions relative to
2
-dimensional cones such that
\eta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
does not cross
\overline{\tau}
.
Consider the subfan
\Gamma
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
consisting of the cones contained in
\overline{\tau}
.
Using the induction hypothesis to
\Gamma
and all the
(n-2)
-dimensional faces of
\tau
,
and after star subdividing
\Gamma
and hence
\Sigma^{\prime}
too,
we may assume that these faces do not cross
\Gamma
.
Now, let
\sigma
be a cone of
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Since
\overline{\tau}
does not cross
\Sigma
,

\sigma\cap\overline{\tau}
is a cone of
\sigma
.
Together with Lemma
D.4
,
we see that
\tau
does not cross
\sigma\cap\overline{\tau}
and hence
\sigma
too.
∎

The following result is a slight adjustment of

[
22
, Theorem 2.4]
(see also
[
51
, pp. 39-40]
).

Proposition D.6
.

Let
\Sigma
and
\Delta
be fans with the same support
\lvert\Sigma\rvert=\lvert\Delta\rvert
and a common cone
\eta\in\Sigma,\Delta
.
Assume that
\Sigma
is smooth.
Then there exist subdivisions of fans
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma,\Delta
such that
\eta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
is obtained by a finite sequence of star subdivisions relative to
2
-dimensional cones.

Proof.

Let
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
be the maximal cones of
\Sigma
.
Assume that the claim holds for any fan whose support agrees with
\sigma_{i}
for some
i
.
Then by induction on
m
,
we obtain a desired
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Hence we reduce to the case where
\Sigma
is a fan in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with support
\mathbb{N}^{n}
.

By Lemma
D.5
, there exists a subdivision
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
obtained by a finite sequence of star subdivisions relative to
2
-dimensional cones such that
\eta\in\Sigma^{\prime}
and every
(n-1)
-dimensional cone of
\Delta
does not cross
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Lemma
D.4
implies that every
n
-dimensional cone of
\Delta
does not cross
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
It follows that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a subdivision of
\Delta
.
∎

Proposition D.7
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then there exists an admissible blow-up
Y\to X
with
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
D.1
,
there exists an admissible blow-up
X^{\prime}\to X
in
\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
\underline{X^{\prime}}
is smooth.
Replacing
X
by
X^{\prime}
,
we may assume that
\underline{X}
is smooth.

Consider the smooth fans
\Sigma
and
\Delta
corresponding to
\underline{X}
and
X-\partial X
.
Let
\alpha(\Sigma,\Delta)
be the set of cones
\sigma
of
\Sigma
not in
\Delta
such that every ray of
\sigma
is in
\Delta
.
If
\alpha(\Sigma,\Delta)=\emptyset
, then we have
X\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
by Proposition
C.35
.
This is the reason why we consider
\alpha(\Sigma,\Delta)
.

Now, let
\sigma
be a cone of
\alpha(\Sigma,\Delta)
having a maximal dimension among the cones of
\alpha(\Sigma,\Delta)
, say
d
.
Consider the star subdivision
\Sigma^{*}(\sigma)
.
Then the center of
\sigma
is not in
\Delta
and hence every
d
-dimensional cone containing that center is not in
\alpha(\Sigma^{*}(\sigma),\Delta)
.
It follows that
\alpha(\Sigma^{*}(\sigma),\Delta)
has one less
d
-dimensional cones than
\alpha(\Sigma^{*}(\sigma),\Delta)
.
Also,
\alpha(\Sigma^{*}(\sigma),\Delta)
does not contain a cone whose dimension is
>d
.
By repeating this process,
we see that there exists a composition of star subdivisions
\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
such that
\alpha(\Sigma^{\prime},\Delta)=\emptyset
,
Then Construction
C.18
yields a desired
Y\to X
.
∎

Proposition D.8
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then there exists proper
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
containing
X
as an open subscheme.

Proof.

The monoid scheme
X
is associated with a smooth fan
\Sigma
.
There exists a complete fan
\Delta
containing
\Sigma
as a subfan by
[
51
, p. 18]
.
Then the pair
(\Delta,\Sigma)
yields
Y\in\mathrm{cSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
such that
\underline{Y}
is proper.
Proposition
D.7
finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition D.9
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then there exists a dividing cover
Y\to X
with
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Proof.

Consider
X^{\sharp}\in\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
obtained by Construction
C.25
,
which also shows that the categories of dividing covers of
X
and
X^{\sharp}
are equivalent.
Let
\Sigma
be the fan such that
X^{\sharp}\simeq\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
.
By Proposition
D.1
,
there exists a subdivision
\Delta\to\Sigma
such that
\Delta
is smooth.
The induced morphism
\mathbb{T}_{\Delta}\to\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
is a dividing cover,
and this corresponds to a dividing cover
Y\to X
in
\mathrm{lSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
From the smoothness of
\Delta
,
we have
\underline{Y}\in\mathrm{Sm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
and hence
Y\in\mathrm{SmlSm}/\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
∎
